Appearances of Inire

Urth list · 230 messages · 2010-06-28 to 2010-07-06

2010urthThe Book of the New SunThe Book of the Long SunJohn WatkinsRyan DunnJerry FriedmanLee BermanAdam ThorntonJosh GellerPaul BAntónio Pedro MarquesGerry QuinnDavid StockhoffJames WynnDave LeblingEugene ZaretskiySon of WitzJeff WilsonDan'l Danehy-OakesRoy C. LackeyDavid Duffy
The thread: The thread asks who Father Inire of The Book of the New Sun really is and whether he appears in disguise across the books. Ryan Dunn, Lee Berman, Jeff Wilson and David Stockhoff proposed candidates including Master Ultan, Rudesind, the boatman, Ossipago and Palaemon, and the exultant-family and time-travel questions spread into long arithmetic debates. António Pedro Marques, Eugene Zaretskiy and Roy Lackey pushed back against over-reading and against Rudesind-as-Inire, while John Watkins argued for the jungle shaman. The thread drifted into tangents on astronaut photos, theodicy and the Whorl, and ended without consensus.
  • Ryan Dunn: Inire is a puppetmaster and disguised figure, possibly Rudesind and the Curators' master
  • Jeff Wilson: Rudesind is Inire's body double; Curators may be his agents
  • John Watkins: Inire is clearly the tiger-shaman or uturuncu of the jungle
  • Roy C. Lackey: Rudesind-as-Inire fails on timeline and Severian's later knowledge
  • António Pedro Marques: rejects reducing the book to one hidden character without a richer reading
  • David Stockhoff: Rudesind cannot be Inire; the monkey description may be literal

Themes: Masks, disguise and shapeshifting, Time loops and causality, Gods, angels and the Increate

2010-06-28 Adam Thornton replying to Ryan Dunn #

Proposes that Inire, described with long arms and red hair, is Master Ultan, the Librarian, and jokingly argues that an appearance before The Color of Magic would require time travel. Replies to Ryan Dunn's list of Inire's appearances, citing the ape-like description and the Librarian role.
proposes · puzzle-solving · theories: Inire is Master Ultan

Bent, long arms, red hair, monkey^Wape-like.

Well, obviously Inire's also Master Ultan. Since he's clearly the Librarian. Obviously, too, time travel is required to have him appear *before* _The Color of Magic_.

Ook.

2010-06-28 Lee Berman #

Proposes that Father Inire appears in many guises across The Book of the New Sun, arguing Rudesind the picture cleaner is one of them and listing further candidates such as Ossipago, Palaemon, the Boatman, Fechin and the jungle guide. Grounds this in Inire's first description as old, bent, monkey-like and drawn to young girls, plus shared traits like folksy speech, boats, art and red hair.
proposes · puzzle-solving · close-textual-reading · theories: Rudesind the picture cleaner is Father Inire; Ossipago and Palaemon are versions of Inire; The Boatman is Father Inire; Fechin is Father Inire

Woo hoo! Well, Borski has also considered Ossipago and Palaemon as versions of Father Inire. I'm not sure about them.

But I thought I was alone in considering the picture cleaner, Rudesind, to perhaps be Inire. I suppose I am the champion/clown prince in finding Father Inire in the most places actually. I haven't posted about that in a few years (under the name bsharp, for some reason that account won't work here any more. Was I banned? yikes!)

My starting point is the first mention of Father Inire where he is described as old, bent and twisted and monkey-like. Rudesind fits the bill in appearance and in his occupation since Father Inire seems to be involved in all museum-oriented things artisan, arts, architecture, contrivances etc.

We also learn that Inire has an attraction to young pretty girls. This helps bring the Boatman, Dorcas' husband, into the pool of candidates. He is old, bent, hangs out in the right place, and speaks in a folksy voice like Rudesind. Boats plus young girls plus folksy talk plus telling "manatee" stories suggests Maxellindis' "uncle" might be in the mix also.

Monkey arms, a thing for young girls and interest in the arts makes Fechin another candidate. Especially since Rudesind's boyhood painting might be a self-portrait (there are paint brushes in the picture). This adds red hair to the mix and opens up all sorts of worm cans. (e.g. I think I know why Agia has auburn hair).

There are the likely possibilities of the cowled servitor of the autarch, the jungle guide and Isangoma. Plus I have to wonder about the odd monkey creatures which seem to peek in on Severian's progress from time to time.

2010-06-28 Ryan Dunn replying to Lee Berman #

Argues that Inire is a puppetmaster who, with Severian, shapes the Severian of The Book of the New Sun toward becoming the New Sun, and that Inire and Ossipago and Palaemon may be multiple people in disguise. Presents the Truman Show comparison and the Atrium of Time moment as evidence, saying coincidences are justified under this reading.
proposes · puzzle-solving · myth-and-structure · theories: Inire is a puppetmaster shaping Severian; Severian lives in parallel timelines; Inire and Severian collude to create the New Sun; Ossipago and Palaemon are Inire in disguise

Yes!

My whole take on Inire, is that he is something of a puppetmaster. We get an in-depth story from Thecla about Inire's mirrors, and how he is a master of the corridors of time. We also get plenty allusion that Severian lives in parallel timelines, none more evident than as Apu Punchau.

The motivation, IMO, would be for Inire and perhaps Severian to be in collusion in trying to shape the Severian (that we follow in BotNS) to become the New Sun. If at first you don't succeed...

Also, if there are seven Severians or more floating around Urth and beyond, and Inire was the master who taught him (assumption) why couldn't there be a dozen or more Inire's coexisting? Why COULDN'T he be more than one character in disguise? I always felt Ossipago and Palaemon to be OBVIOUSLY in disguise, and both VERY critical in setting Severian's course.

When I read the text with this in mind, all of the "coincidences" are suddenly justified and warranted, almost requisite. Also, when I read it this way, I feel like that "bubble" Severian is in, is very much intentional. It's sort of like Peter Weir's "Truman Show" with Jim Carrey, in a way. With Inire playing Ed Harriss's role.

I remember Severian senses a shadow of himself at the end of the book, when he returns to the Atrium of Time. As though the Severian from the beginning was only moments away, perhaps overlapping since time behaves weird there.

...ryan

2010-06-28 Jeff Wilson replying to Ryan Dunn #

Questions whether Inire is a time traveler himself, noting that the text names four characters who travel in time without artifice and five artifices enabling it, yet Inire is never shown passing through them. Grounds the point in a count of the time-travel devices and characters and a recollection of the book's episodes, responding to Ryan Dunn's claim that Inire and Severian are known time travelers.
questions · chronology-and-plot-logic · close-textual-reading · theories: Inire is a time traveler

Is Inire known to travel in time, himself? Or is he the station master?

IIRC, there are four characters who may travel in time without artifice: Severian, Juturna, the Green Man, and Tzadkerbell.

There are about five artifices that allow time travel: the Ship, Inire's mirrors, the various dimensional architectures, the portals in Yesod, and FB&O's saucer. Despite Inire's association with and mastery of most or all of these, he's not documented passing through them into other times that I recall.

(His Botanical Gardens technically reach into other times in other cycles but there's no opportunity to touch back into Urthly history of future there I can see.)

Whole message, with quotes · original
> If so, why not suspect two known time-travelers in this text, Severian and
> Inire (in no particular order). What is the endgame for them? To bring the
> New Sun, correct?

2010-06-28 James Wynn replying to Lee Berman #

Proposes that red hair in the Sun cycle is deliberately linked to Typhon, citing werewolf lore and the Typhonians of the Osiris cult. Replies to Lee Berman's suggestion that the painter Fechin, whose works look nearly photographic, may link Rudesind's self-portrait and Agia's hair.
proposes · source-and-allusion · puzzle-solving · theories: Red hair in the Sun cycle is intentionally associated with Typhon; Agia's auburn hair links to Typhon

"We've a Fechin here that shows three girls dressing another one with flowers that's so real you expect the bees to come out of it." ~ Shadow of the Torturer

Nicolai Ivanovich Fechin was an actual early 20th century painter. He's classified as an Impressionist. But the color and detail of many of his paintings are nearly photorealistic, and the eye-popping color often gives the sense of three-dimensional depth.

I have a theory that red-hair in the Sun Cycle is an intentional association with Typhon in some way. Red-hair is signifier of being a werewolf, and the red-haired sacrificial victims in the Osiris cult were called "Typhonians".

~ J. u+16b9

2010-06-28 David Stockhoff replying to Adam Thornton (IO) #

Argues that Inire could appear as several different characters across the books, perhaps including one who is blind, and that the multiple names would help him keep track. Responds to Adam Thornton's claim that Inire is Master Ultan by warning that applying the principle of conservation of characters too broadly would make almost any male character Inire, even Severian.
disputes · puzzle-solving · close-textual-reading · theories: Inire appears as multiple characters; Inire is Master Ultan

It's one thing to suggest multiple Inires. He could probably handle that. And if so, then one of them could be blind without them all being blind. And I suppose the multiple names would be so he could keep himself straight.

Also, since Father Inire himself never appears, it follows that he must appear as something other than Father Inire.

But I agree with what I think is your skepticism: there is the principle of conservation of characters, but this is overapplication. At this rate, anyone (male) could be Inire after several hundred years, even Severian if he works on his tan.

Whole message, with quotes · original

2010-06-28 Roy C. Lackey replying to David Stockhoff #

Argues that Inire could be a female character as well as a male one, citing the shapeshifting Ossipago, the Agia family tree, and the dead and living figures he has supposedly become. Responds to David Stockhoff's skepticism about over-applying the conservation of characters by joking that Severian's relations make everyone Inire.
disputes · puzzle-solving · theology-and-religion · theories: Inire appears across many characters in the books; Inire could be female as well as male

Why limit Inire to male characters? If he can be a fraking robot (Ossipago) and monkey his way up Agia's family tree (which, according to some, makes him related to Severian), and be both dead characters (Boatman, Maxellindis' uncle) and living ones, a change of gender is no obstacle. And since Severian only screws his relatives, by logical extension "All You Zombies" is us and we are Inire, but we are not alone.

-Roy

2010-06-28 Ryan Dunn replying to David Stockhoff #

Argues that the convenient coincidences in The Book of the New Sun are deliberate design, and proposes that Inire is a time-traveler disguised among characters who help Severian along his journey. Grounds this in Rudesind's misdirection possibly saving Severian from an armiger ambush, and in Severian himself being a known time-traveler, asking whether the reader would accept Inire's purposeful presence.
proposes · puzzle-solving · myth-and-structure · theories: Inire is a time-traveler disguised among helpers; Coincidences in BotNS are authorial design

Personally, I think all of the coincidences or overly convenient happenings in BotNS are a product of design, not chance, or an easy way out for Wolfe. Would you agree?

If so, why not suspect two known time-travelers in this text, Severian and Inire (in no particular order). What is the endgame for them? To bring the New Sun, correct?

Not sure why it is such a stretch to see Inire in disguise, helping the way. In fact, Rudesind giving different directions may have saved him from (I dunno) an armiger's ambush? Not drawing random conclusions, just saying there would be a "reason" for Inire to plant himself along Severian's journey, no?

...ryan

2010-06-28 Ryan Dunn replying to Roy C. Lackey #

Argues that Father Inire is a monkeylike figure whose recurring appearances across The Book of the New Sun, including Fechin and a red-bearded monkey, suggest a hidden boy-in-a-bubble context for the whole story. Supports the case by quoting passages from The Shadow of the Torturer, The Sword of the Lictor and The Claw of the Conciliator describing monkeylike men and monkeys.
proposes · close-textual-reading · puzzle-solving · theories: Inire is a boy in a bubble

I wouldn't count any of this theorizing as a conspiracy theory, if that is what you're implying here.

The idea of a boy in a bubble is very plausible, and could be the context for this entire story. How many side-stories involving plays, puppets or other theatrics are here in the text to serve as beacons?

Father Inire clearly described as monkeylike...

"More fantastic still were the tales of his vizier, the famous Father Inire, who looked like a monkey and was the oldest man in the world." (SotT)

An old, monkeylike man pointing Severian on his way...

"When I admitted I did not, he scrambled down from the ladder like an aged monkey, seeming all arms and legs and wrinkled neck; his hands were as long as my feet, the crooked fingers laced with blue veins." (SotT)

A painting of a painter who looks monkeylike, with red-hair...

"He was the worst of us all, that Fechin. A tall, wild boy with red hair on his hands, on his arms. Like a monkey's arms, so that if you saw them reaching around the corner to take something, you'd think, except for the size, that it was a monkey taking it." (SotL)

Oh, and here's a red-bearded monkey spying on Severian, because why not? We never see Inire with his socks off, after all...

"Birds unknown to me called overhead, and once a monkey who might, save for his four hands, have been a wizened, red-bearded man in fur, spied on me from a fork as high as a spire." (CotC)

Those are just a few I pulled. The crissing and crossing of things in the book is impossible to ignore, however over the top it may seem. No?

...ryan

2010-06-28 David Stockhoff replying to Ryan Dunn #

Agrees with Ryan Dunn that Inire, a suspected time-traveler alongside Severian, must appear in disguise or unannounced since he never shows his face conventionally. Builds on the thread's argument that BotNS coincidences are designed, offering Inire's absence of a conventional face as the grounds for the disguise idea.
supports · puzzle-solving · theories: Inire appears in disguise or unannounced; Inire is a time-traveler like Severian

Of course. And as I said, the fact that Inire never shows his face in the conventional manner means he must appear in disguise or unannounced.

Whole message, with quotes · original

the conventional manner means he must appear in disguise or unannounced.

2010-06-28 David Stockhoff replying to Dan'l Danehy-Oakes #

But there will be no "they" left. They will all be us.

2010-06-28 David Stockhoff replying to Ryan Dunn #

Argues that the monkey description of Father Inire is probably literal, since exultants are not really human and may have monkey faces, and questions whether exultant height is natural or bred. Grounds this in the text's descriptions of exultant families dwindling and in Palaemon's blindness, cautioning against reading every monkey as a clue to a bubble-boy theory.
disputes · close-textual-reading · chronology-and-plot-logic · theories: Inire has a literal monkey face; exultants are a dying race of rulers

Surely sometimes a monkey is just a monkey.

It is at very least a theme. We certainly do need to note any time "monkey" is used to describe a character. It may be Typhonian, it may be a trickster. Typhon himself is a bit of a trickster. And Severian is certainly in a kind of bubble. But Palaemon? Isn't he almost blind?

Also, recall that exultants are very tall and long-limbed. I assume that Inire, who is after all not really human, would have a monkey face---that that is what is meant by that description. Limbs alone may not be enough. Is he red-haired?

Does anyone know whether exultants have simply retained our unnatural 21st century height while everyone else shrank with the new ice age, or exultants were actually made taller by breeding or other means? There is a detailed discussion of exultant families and their dwindling---this suggests to me a dying race of world-rulers, not a created class of freaks. Are any units of measurement or comparisons used that could tell us?

2010-06-28 Jeff Wilson replying to Ryan Dunn #

Argues that the exultant families in The Book of the New Sun are genetically taller and further enhanced by hormones or glands taken from khaibits, and proposes that the list of families may not map one-to-one onto the 746 pages. Reasons from a head-shaping image and the royal analogy of the Windsor and Saxe-Coburg lines, noting that marriage can merge several families, responding to David Stockhoff's page-count arithmetic posted in reply to Ryan Dunn.
proposes · source-and-allusion · close-textual-reading · theories: Exultant height comes from genetics and khaibit glands

They're both. Exultants have greater height genetically, and enhance it further by harvesting hormones or actual glands from their khaibits. This modification for greater height may be a tradition going back as far as the stone town:

http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Head_Shaping.jpg

2 x 746 = 1492, but that may not be a one for one correspondence, as one family may include others by marriage, like Prince Charles being a Windsor as well as a Saxe-Coburg-Gotha and a
Schleswig-Holstein-Sonderburg-Gl?cksburg, which are themselves obviously made of other families, each with their own laws about succession and inheritance.

2010-06-28 Ryan Dunn replying to David Stockhoff #

Argues that Gurloes in the Book of the New Sun shows Severian the vastness of the exultant families still surviving, not a dwindling race, by calculating roughly 3,000 living bloodlines from the 746-page book. Responds to David Stockhoff's claim that the discussion of dwindling exultant families suggests a dying race of world-rulers, using the quoted passage from the Gurloes library scene as evidence.
disputes · close-textual-reading

Yes, there used to be more families over the course of histories, but it seemed to me that Gurloes was impressing upon Severian the VASTNESS of the exultant families still going, no?

There are nearly 3,000 exultant blood lines still going, based on this math...

.

"He turned, and after searching the disorderly shelves behind his chair produced a squat book. 'Have you any notion how many exulted families there are? This lists only the ones that are still going. A compendium of the extinguished ones would take an encyclopedia, I suppose. I've extinguished a few of them myself.'

He laughed and I laughed with him.

'It gives about half a page to each. There are seven hundred and forty- six pages.'"

.

...ryan

2010-06-28 John Watkins replying to Jeff Wilson #

Argues that Inire may be the uturuncu, the shaman in the jungle, based on a passage in The Citadel of the Autarch where Severian says the master was in the northern jungles trying to rescue his predecessor. Reasons that this clue invites a reread of the text and raises doubts about Severian's perfect memory and Inire's stalking, rather than being mere motif or red herring.
proposes · close-textual-reading · puzzle-solving · theories: Inire is the uturuncu shaman

Well, as Dan Rabin pointed out on this list, Severian implies pretty strongly in Autarch that he knows Inire was the uturuncu:

"We even know that your master was with us in the jungles of the north, where he tried until it was too late to rescue my predecessor."

If that's right--if we were supposed to pick up that the bent old shaman in the jungle was Inire--then we're pretty directly being challenged to reread the text from the beginning looking for the guy (and to question whether Severian's "perfect" memory is consistent with Inire's stalking.) I buy motifs, and I buy red herrings, but I don't buy that there's that broad clue and a million monkeylike references without there being at least a little fire.

2010-06-28 Roy C. Lackey replying to David Stockhoff #

Argues against a theory that Inire manipulates Severian through parallel timelines, asking how his supposed deeds fit his known whereabouts as painter, vizier and lake-prober. Answers David Stockhoff's height question with Wolfe's stated 6'1" for Severian and Ultan's height, then cites Thecla's mirror story and Apu Punchau as the evidence for the theory.
disputes · chronology-and-plot-logic · theories: Inire manipulates Severian across parallel timelines; Severian lives in parallel timelines as Apu Punchau; Inire is a master of the corridors of time

Yes. According to Wolfe, Severian is 6'1". The exultant Ultan was a head and a half taller than Severian.

an in-depth story from Thecla about Inire's mirrors, and how he is a master of the corridors of time. We also get plenty allusion that Severian lives in parallel timelines, none more evident than as Apu Punchau. collusion in trying to shape the Severian (that we follow in BotNS) to become the New Sun. If at first you don't succeed...

And how would being a famous painter two generations before Severian's time have furthered the coming of the New Sun?

How would probing a lake for forty years looking for the body of Severian's grandmother have helped? Where did Inire find the time, since he was the Old Autarch's vizier during those years? Not to mention that the old boatman died but Inire was still there as Severian's vizier for another ten years.

-Roy

2010-06-28 Ryan Dunn replying to Jeff Wilson #

Argues that Gurloes's book, printed on both sides of each sheet, would list four families per page, which bears on the arithmetic of Jeff Wilson's family count. Responds to Jeff Wilson's point that families may overlap through marriage by treating the book's entries as house names regardless of intermarriage.
disputes · puzzle-solving · theories: Gurloes's book lists house names not individual families

Jeff,

Just pointing out, that Gurloes's book is likely printed on both sides, which means one half of one side of one sheet = 4 families per page.

But yeah, could be some cross-over, though I took the book to be house names, irrelevant of the cross-pollenation.

...ryan

2010-06-29 Ryan Dunn replying to Roy C. Lackey #

Argues that Inire appears in disguise in several characters, including the boatman, Rudesind, and possibly Ossipago, and suspects Ossipago and Palaemon share a nature. Responds to Roy Lackey's objections about Inire's timeline by conceding the boatman was not Inire while pressing the monkey-like description and Gyoll encounter as clues.
proposes · puzzle-solving · close-textual-reading · theories: Inire appears disguised as Rudesind; Ossipago may be Inire; Ossipago and Palaemon are the same kind of being

Do you disagree then that Inire appears to us in any form during the course of the books?

I never thought the boatman was Inire, btw. But the boatman seems to be the same man at Gyoll who asks Roche (?) whether Severian mentioned a woman after his drowning.

Rudesind could be Inire, in order to get Severian to Ultan.

Not sure about Fechin (maybe too deep for me).

But why go to the trouble to make a point of a character having monkey-like qualities, which is clearly how Inire is described (at least second or third hand)?

Regarding Ossipago, he may/may not be Inire. I do however suspect he and Palaemon are of the same ilk if not the same being outright.

...ryan

Not sure about Fechin (maybe too deep for me).

But why go to the trouble to make a point of a character having monkey-like qualities, which is clearly how Inire is described (at least second or third hand)?

Regarding Ossipago, he may/may not be Inire. I do however suspect he and Palaemon are of the same ilk if not the same being outright.

...ryan

2010-06-29 Ryan Dunn replying to John Watkins #

Disputes John Watkins's suggestion that Inire's monkey-like description is a red herring, arguing that Wolfe does not use red herrings and that the device is too lazy and convenient. Accepts that the trait may be a motif but recommends dropping the descriptor for a different one, answering Watkins's quoted question about why the trait is stressed.
disputes · close-textual-reading · theories: Inire's monkey-like traits are not a red herring

Also, I argue against/reject the idea of red herrings in a Gene Wolfe novel.

Motif maybe, but a red herring just feels too lazy and convenient. Easier to just leave it alone, find a different descriptor, then.

...ryan

2010-06-29 Roy C. Lackey replying to Ryan Dunn #

Argues that the boatman, the cowled guide, the jungle shaman and the ape-dog in the lazaret are not Inire, and that Inire's ape-like traits do not make Palaemon or Ossipago the same being. Defends the view that Rudesind may have lied about Inire's letter, but that Severian would have discovered Inire's role over the ten years before writing, and says Palaemon is clearly human.
disputes · close-textual-reading · chronology-and-plot-logic · theories: Inire appears in many disguises across the books; Palaemon is an ordinary aging man; Rudesind lied about Inire's letter

I think he was the guy in a cowel who led Sev out of the House Absolute and back to the theater troupe. And of course that he was the shaman in the jungle who tried to rescue the Old Autarch. And he may have been the ape-dog thing that looked in on Sev in the lazaret after the avern duel.

But Lee does, and we had this whole monkey battle a few years ago.

whether Severian mentioned a woman after his drowning.

Many people have thought so, but there are some who don't buy it.

Yeah, and Rudesind could have lied about the letter from Inire at the end of CITADEL. Of course he would have been lying to the new autarch, the autarch Inire would be vizier to for the next ten years. A rather foolish thing to do, it seems to me. And Sev may not be as smart as some people like to think, but how stupid would he have to be not to have found Inire out in those ten years that went by before he wrote the manuscript that detailed that meeting?

That old battle. Again. <g> I haven't changed my mind and neither has Lee.

monkey-like qualities, which is clearly how Inire is described (at least second or third hand)?
Palaemon are of the same ilk if not the same being outright.

Ossipago is a machine. Palaemon is very, very human, an old man who aged visibly in the six months between Sev's exile and return to the tower as autarch. That one scene near the end of CITADEL is enough to show that clearly. Sev was able to see in the old man's face the youth he had been. And he eats and drinks wine, sighs, makes facial expressions, etc. He is a man.

-Roy

2010-06-29 Lee Berman replying to Lee Berman #

Proposes that Father Inire may be a shape-changing being who appears as Ultan, Catherine's father, Dorcas's husband, with the Cumaean as his female counterpart. Grounds this in Tzadkiel's shape-changing and limits on resurrection in The Urth of the New Sun, the Boatman's motive to revive his wife, and a Genesis 6 parallel between the Flood and fallen angels.
proposes · puzzle-solving · theology-and-religion · theories: Inire may be Catherine's father; Inire may be Dorcas's husband; Cumaean is female version of Inire; Boatman may be restoring his dead wife

Yes, Ultan is a curator. And like some other candidates, Ultan's eyes are obscured. I think the dark haired woman in the grave is some version of Catherine. Why does Severian see her face in Ultan's? Could he be Catherine's father? If Inire is also Dorcas' husband, he would appear as grandfather to Severian on both sides of the family. Why IS he called "Father" Inire? I can't recall any religious association.

If he is Isangoma, who resembles the jungle shaman, then I'd say, yes.

In UotNS we are rather explicitly shown that Tzadkiel can change size, shape gender and plurality, so I agree with Roy's statement. If there is a female version of Father Inire, the most likely candidate is Cumaean. One is Father, the other is The Mother. We hear them paired as alike but they are never quite together. Inire has his monkey essence, the Cumaean has her snake essence. I don't know if they are supposed to be borne of the same original entity or not but they could be.

Given the Tzadkiel model, there is no reason there can't be multiple versions of the same being running around manipulating Severian's life and travels. We are told that not even Tzadkiel can resurrect a dead person. So, I suspect, neither can Father Inire (or the Cumaean, by herself). Perhaps it wasn't a coincidence the boatman just happened to be there on the lake when Severian showed up. How else could he restore his long dead wife?

Tzadkiel is an angel (who never comes to Urth). What is Inire?

Genesis 6:1-8 seems to suggest that the purpose of The Flood was to cleanse the earth and humanity of a corruption brought on by the mating of fallen angels and human women, producing giants, demons, etc. Severian's resistance to the seduction of Juturna is significant. What is the purpose of the Flood of Urth? The same as the Biblical Flood, I think.

I suspect that Wolfe is making the point that the fallen angels of Judeo-Christianity bear a striking resemblance to the gods and monsters of pagan mythology and are, perhaps, one and the same. Incestuous, large sized, animal essenced, shape-changing, other "magic" abilities, mating with humans, the pattern is fairly consistent. (Hephaestus is an artisan, bent and twisted, sometimes depicted with red hair).

One more candidate for Father Inire- Cyriaca's book-loving "uncle" who seems to know so much about the inner workings of the Autarchy and the history of humanity and the universe. And Cyriaca does share some characteristics with Catherine. Cyriaca's dark hair paired with a red-headed father might produce the chestnut color we find in Agia and Agilus. Thus the origin of the incestuous curse which seems to plague Severian's family- the Father.

Yes. I once read a Gene Wolfe interview in which he said he was unhappy with his status as an only child and spent much of his youth scouring the faces of strangers for signs of hidden family and lost kinship. This is more explicitly themed in Fifth Head of Cerberus but I think it makes a significant appearance in New Sun also.

(p.s. the "battles" over these issues from a few years ago were invaluable to me in helping clarify my interpretations. I truly appreciate all those who participated and helped me)

2010-06-29 Jeff Wilson replying to Ryan Dunn #

Argues that Gurloes's page count in the Claw of the Conciliator Inire census passage cannot be taken as exact, because a numbered page may be one side of a leaf and each page holds only two halves. Works the arithmetic to keep the family estimate near 1500 and notes the figure sets a limit on precision for other numbers in the book.
disputes · chronology-and-plot-logic

'It gives about half a page to each. There are seven hundred and forty- six pages.'

Unless Gurloes is like Rainman, he got the page count from the last numbered page. Traditionally there is one numbered page on either side of a leaf. But whatever a "page" is, there are 746 of them, and each one can only have two halves, so the accommodated number of families remains approx 1500.

Ordinarily I wouldn't bother digressing on this, but it does establish something of a maximum degree of precision for figures given in the book, and I'm warming up to a new estimate on the Wall's height based on Ouen's comments where it might be important.

Whole message, with quotes · original

2010-06-29 Jeff Wilson replying to John Watkins #

Explains the printing terms signature, leaf and page, and counts the signatures and leaves in the Book Club edition of The Sword of the Lictor. Replies to John Watkins's remark that people call one face of a sheet a page, adding a bibliographic correction.
informs

Technically, most published books have several pages printed on a large sheet of paper that is called a signature when it is folded for attachment to others to form a volume. They are (or were) then cut into separate leaves to give the reader access to the pages otherwise concealed within the folds.

The Book Club edition of SotL, for instance, has 16 signatures by my count, accounting for the 279 pages of story, 1 about the author, and 8 front matter if they alternate 10 leaves as in the first signature with 8 leaves as in the last. There are two more protective blank leaves of sturdier paper which are actually part of the end papers glued inside the covers.

Whole message, with quotes · original
On 6/29/2010 1:08 PM, John Watkins wrote:
> ...and almost everyone in the world refers to a "page" as being a single
> face of the paper, not a single sheet of paper.  Which there are
> different numbers on the opposite sides of the sheet.

Technically, most published books have several pages printed on a large 
sheet of paper that is called a signature when it is folded for 
attachment to others to form a volume. They are (or were) then cut into 
separate leaves to give the reader access to the pages otherwise 
concealed within the folds.

The Book Club edition of SotL, for instance, has 16 signatures by my 
count, accounting for the 279 pages of story, 1 about the author, and 8 
front matter if they alternate 10 leaves as in the first signature with 
8 leaves as in the last. There are two more protective blank leaves of 
sturdier paper which are actually part of the end papers glued inside 
the covers.

2010-06-29 David Stockhoff replying to Jeff Wilson #

Correct. Two pages make a leaf.

Whole message, with quotes · original
Correct. Two pages make a leaf.

Jeff Wilson wrote:
> On 6/28/2010 10:22 PM, Ryan Dunn wrote:
>> Jeff,
>>
>> Just pointing out, that Gurloes's book is likely printed on both 
>> sides, which means one half of one side of one sheet = 4 families per 
>> page.
>
> 'It gives about half a page to each. There are seven hundred and 
> forty- six pages.'
>
> Unless Gurloes is like Rainman, he got the page count from the last 
> numbered page. Traditionally there is one numbered page on either side 
> of a leaf. But whatever a "page" is, there are 746 of them, and each 
> one can only have two halves, so the accommodated number of families 
> remains approx 1500.
>
> Ordinarily I wouldn't bother digressing on this, but it does establish 
> something of a maximum degree of precision for figures given in the 
> book, and I'm warming up to a new estimate on the Wall's height based 
> on Ouen's comments where it might be important.
>

2010-06-29 David Stockhoff replying to Lee Berman #

Argues that Inire and the Cumaean form a Mother and Father pair, and that Wolfe builds his gods on real-world deities, so such pairings are to be expected. Responds to Lee Berman's speculation that Inire might be Dorcas' husband and Severian's grandfather, and his question why Inire is called Father, by offering a general point about Wolfe's divine figures rather than a specific identification.
disputes · myth-and-structure · source-and-allusion · theories: Inire and the Cumaean form a Mother and Father pair

Wolfe bases all his "gods" on "real" gods. Yes, there does seem to be a pairing of Mother and Father in Inire and the Cumaean, but then there would be.

Whole message, with quotes · original
Wolfe bases all his "gods" on "real" gods. Yes, there does seem to be a 
pairing of Mother and Father in Inire and the Cumaean, but then there 
would be.

Lee Berman wrote:
>> Well, obviously Inire's also Master Ultan.  Since he's clearly the
>> Librarian.
>>     
>  
> Yes, Ultan is a curator. And like some other candidates, Ultan's eyes 
> are obscured. I think the dark haired woman in the grave is some version
> of Catherine. Why does Severian see her face in Ultan's? Could he be 
> Catherine's father? If Inire is also Dorcas' husband, he would appear
> as grandfather to Severian on both sides of the family. Why IS he called
> "Father" Inire? I can't recall any religious association.
>  
>
>   
>> Is Inire known to travel in time, himself? 
>>     
>  
> If he is Isangoma, who resembles the jungle shaman, then I'd say, yes.
>
>  
>   
>> Why limit Inire to male characters? If he can be a fraking robot (Ossipago)
>> and monkey his way up Agia's family tree (which, according to some, makes
>> him related to Severian), and be both dead characters (Boatman, Maxellindis'
>> uncle) and living ones, a change of gender is no obstacle.
>>     
>  
> In UotNS we are rather explicitly shown that Tzadkiel can change size, shape
> gender and plurality, so I agree with Roy's statement. If there is a female 
> version of Father Inire, the most likely candidate is Cumaean. One is Father,
> the other is The Mother. We hear them paired as alike but they are never quite 
> together. Inire has his monkey essence, the Cumaean has her snake essence. I
> don't know if they are supposed to be borne of the same original entity or not
> but they could be.
>  
> Given the Tzadkiel model, there is no reason there can't be multiple versions
> of the same being running around manipulating Severian's life and travels. We
> are told that not even Tzadkiel can resurrect a dead person. So, I suspect, 
> neither can Father Inire (or the Cumaean, by herself). Perhaps it wasn't a
> coincidence the boatman just happened to be there on the lake when Severian 
> showed up. How else could he restore his long dead wife?
>  
> Tzadkiel is an angel (who never comes to Urth). What is Inire? 
>  
> Genesis 6:1-8 seems to suggest that the purpose of The Flood was to cleanse 
> the earth and humanity of a corruption brought on by the mating of fallen 
> angels and human women, producing giants, demons, etc. Severian's resistance to
> the seduction of Juturna is significant. What is the purpose of the Flood
> of Urth? The same as the Biblical Flood, I think.
>  
> I suspect that Wolfe is making the point that the fallen angels of 
> Judeo-Christianity bear a striking resemblance to the gods and monsters
> of pagan mythology and are, perhaps, one and the same. Incestuous, large sized, 
> animal essenced, shape-changing, other "magic" abilities, mating with humans, the
> pattern is fairly consistent. (Hephaestus is an artisan, bent and twisted, 
> sometimes depicted with red hair).
>  
> One more candidate for Father Inire- Cyriaca's book-loving "uncle" who seems to 
> know so much about the inner workings of the Autarchy and the history of humanity
> and the universe. And Cyriaca does share some characteristics with Catherine.  
> Cyriaca's dark hair paired with a red-headed father might produce the chestnut color
> we find in Agia and Agilus. Thus the origin of the incestuous curse which seems to
> plague Severian's family- the Father.
>  
>   
>> Severian only screws his relatives, by logical extension "All You Zombies"
>> is us and we are Inire, but we are not alone.
>>     
>
> Yes. I once read a Gene Wolfe interview in which he said he was unhappy with his
> status as an only child and spent much of his youth scouring the faces of strangers
> for signs of hidden family and lost kinship. This is more explicitly themed in 
> Fifth Head of Cerberus but I think it makes a significant appearance in New Sun also.
>  
> (p.s. the "battles" over these issues from a few years ago were invaluable to me in 
> helping clarify my interpretations. I truly appreciate all those who participated and 
> helped me)
>
>
>
>
>
>

2010-06-29 Jeff Wilson replying to David Stockhoff #

Explains how Severian could have been kept in the torturers' guild, arguing that children arriving with arrested clients are sized and distributed, leaving no later chance to reclaim them. Answers David Stockhoff's question about Severian's mother giving birth under torture, citing Severian's account and noting the Seekers will not release children without orders.
answers · close-textual-reading

Sev mentions children who arrive with clients are sized and distributed accordingly. If there is no Dad or other next of kin available to take the client's child at the time of the arrest, there may be no later opportunity to claim them from the soldiers, and the Seekers certainly will not release them without orders.

Whole message, with quotes · original
> Right, that does make sense. And if Severian were the exultant norm,
> that would be one thing---but he's not. Presumably he's half-exultant.
> I'm sure someone has worked all that out.
>
> Would Severian's mother have had to give birth while she was in the
> hands of the torturers for him to have been kept and raised in the
> guild, since his father supposedly is alive?

Sev mentions children who arrive with clients are sized and distributed
accordingly. If there is no Dad or other next of kin available to take the
client's child at the time of the arrest, there may be no later
opportunity to claim them from the soldiers, and the Seekers certainly
will not release them without orders.

2010-06-29 Gerry Quinn replying to David Stockhoff #

Points out that a character named Jonas said the old families are the newest of all, arguing this implies the exultants are the ones who differ from ordinary humans rather than having kept a height they once had. Responds to David Stockhoff's question about whether exultants were bred taller or simply retained their height while others shrank.
disputes · close-textual-reading · theories: Exultants were bred or made taller rather than retaining height

From: "David Stockhoff" <[email]>

Didn't Jonas say "those old families are the newest of all"? This suggests that it is the exultants who differ.

- Gerry Quinn

Whole message, with quotes · original
From: "David Stockhoff" <[email]>

> Does anyone know whether exultants have simply retained our unnatural 21st 
> century height while everyone else shrank with the new ice age, or 
> exultants were actually made taller by breeding or other means? There is a 
> detailed discussion of exultant families and their dwindling---this 
> suggests to me a dying race of world-rulers, not a created class of 
> freaks. Are any units of measurement or comparisons used that could tell 
> us?

Didn't Jonas say "those old families are the newest of all"?  This suggests 
that it is the exultants who differ.

- Gerry Quinn

2010-06-29 David Stockhoff replying to Gerry Quinn #

Argues that Severian, as a half-exultant, is not the exultant norm, and asks whether his mother had to give birth while held by the torturers for him to be raised in the guild despite his father being alive. Responds to Gerry Quinn's point that Jonas's 'those old families are the newest of all' suggests the exultants are what differ.
questions · close-textual-reading · chronology-and-plot-logic · theories: Severian is half-exultant

Right, that does make sense. And if Severian were the exultant norm, that would be one thing---but he's not. Presumably he's half-exultant. I'm sure someone has worked all that out.

Would Severian's mother have had to give birth while she was in the hands of the torturers for him to have been kept and raised in the guild, since his father supposedly is alive?

Whole message, with quotes · original

2010-06-29 Roy C. Lackey replying to Lee Berman #

Argues that Father Inire was a Hierodule, though the books never state it outright. Builds the case from Inire's remarks about the Cumaean, Baldanders' claim that Hierodules live about as long as a dog, Barbatus's comment about knowing Severian half his life, and Sev's note on Inire's long life.
proposes · close-textual-reading · puzzle-solving · theories: Inire was a Hierodule; Severian Tzadkiel is a Hierogrammate

Huh. And I thought I might have been too sarcastic.

The Cumaean was a Hierodule, so Inire had told Sev (URTH, 357). Famulimus and Barbatus nodded agreement when he said that in the tomb.

Tzadkiel is a Hierogrammate. The Hierogrammates created the Hierodules to serve them. Hierodules are short-lived, according to Baldanders, living only about as long as a dog. That statement is indirectly confirmed by Barbatus when he said that "We've known you half our lives now, Severian, . . ." (URTH, 356) They had known and advised Sev for the ten years he had been autarch before leaving for Yesod.

In the first paragraph of chapter XXXVIII in CITADEL, Sev wrote: ". . . old Father Inire, alive so long beyond the span of his short-lived kind . . ."

It is never directly stated in the Urth Cycle that Inire is a Hierodule, but when you put these clues together, the implication is that Inire was a Hierodule, regardless of how he may have lived so much longer than he should have.

Hierodules do not have the power and abilities of a Hierogrammate

-Roy

Whole message, with quotes · original

2010-06-30 Lee Berman replying to Lee Berman #

Argues that Inire's portals and the hierodules should be read as lower-tiered angels within a Biblical hierarchy, not as gods or aliens, and that Tzadkiel can be both an angel and a Hierogrammate. Responds to Roy's skepticism and Brunian's objection about the word "gods" by citing angel-hierarchy excerpts and suggesting Wolfe explored pagan confusion of angels with gods.
disputes · theology-and-religion · allegory-and-typology · theories: Hierodules are lower-tiered angels; Tzadkiel is both angel and Hierogrammate; Inire's portals mirror Tzadkiel's portals

Roy-

That is your value Roy! Even in sarcasm and skepticism you are very helpful as a sounding board. Your comment did force me to consider the gender aspect of shape changing a bit further. Improving my personal understanding of Wolfe books is a self-indulgent exercise but if putting it in print provides food for thought or fodder for amusement or even derision in others, I am content with any of that.

In trying to understand an SF story such as this I find it to be more fiction than science. So I expect metaphor and allusion to have a more important role than, say, a book on astronomy. Thus I have trouble with an argument that a character with the angelic name Tzadkiel is not an angel but is rather a Hierogrammate. Why not both?

Since Biblical angels are arranged in a tiered hierarchy, why can't hierodules be lower-tiered angels in our story? For me, Severian's recognition that Father Inire's portals are a lesser version of Tzadkiel's portals is a way of expressing the relationship. And I find that recognizing "fallen angels" is essential to my understanding of this story. But I know that is not the case for everyone.

brunians-

David stockton did use quotes to address your concern. You are using the lower-case g for your "gods". Is this not sufficient?

Is it possible that ancient pagans confused aliens/angels (fallen or not) with gods, fairies, monsters etc.? Or, more to the point, is it worth considering that Gene Wolfe has explored this possibility in his writing?

[some excerpts from articles about angel heirarchy]-

[some excerpts from articles about angel heirarchy]-
 
>The Dominions (lat. dominatio, pl. dominationes), also known as the Hashmallim,
>hold the task of regulating the duties of lower angels. It is only with extreme
>rarity that the angelic lords make themselves physically known to humans.  The 
>Dominions are believed to look like divinely beautiful humans with a pair of 
>feathered wings, much like the common representation of Angels.
 
>In rabbinic writings Zadkiel belongs to the order of Hashmallim (equated with the
>Dominations or Dominions), and considered by some sources to be chief of that order
>In Maseket Azilut, Zadkiel/Hesediel is listed as co-chief with Gabriel.
 
>The Principalities (lat. principatus, pl. principat?s) are shown wearing a crown
>and carrying a sceptre. Their duty also is said to be to carry out the orders
>given to them by the Dominions and bequeath blessings to the material world.
>Their task is to oversee groups of people.They are the educators and guardians
>of the realm of earth both individuals, as well as groups. As beings related to
>the world of the germinal ideas, they are said to inspire living things to many
>things such as art or science.

2010-06-30 James Wynn replying to António Pedro Marques #

Argues that the Hierogrammates' created beings, including Ossipago, were originally robots or androids and probably still are, extending António Pedro Marques's point that the word hierodule means slave. Builds on the etymology of 'slave' by noting that 'robot' carries the same sense, so the hierodules may be artificial servants.
proposes · source-and-allusion · theories: Hierodules were originally robots or androids; Ossipago is a robot

Another word meaning "slave" is "robot". Ossipago is obviously a robot, but I suppose that the "race" created by the Hierogrammates were originally all robots/androids. Personally, I presume they all are still.

2010-06-30 Jeff Wilson replying to James Wynn #

Responds to James Wynn's claim that the Hierogrammates' created beings were originally robots by saying the idea makes sense, since it avoids reliance on unreliable fleshy creatures. Notes that Tzadkiel and especially Alpheta seem carnal, which complicates the robot reading of the angelic beings.
supports · close-textual-reading · theories: Hierogrammates' created beings were originally robots

That makes a certain amount of sense - it avoids the problems of depending on unreliable fleshy creatures, which could appeal to angels as beings of the spirit, but Tzadkeil and especially Alpheta seem to be carnal beings as well.

2010-06-30 James Wynn replying to Jeff Wilson #

Argues that robots can be built of flesh as well as metal, or tin covered with a fleshy facade, so the bodily nature of angelic figures like Tzadkeil and Alpheta need not rule out their being spirit beings. Responds to Jeff Wilson's quoted claim that angels as beings of the spirit would avoid unreliable fleshy creatures, and dismisses the differences between such creatures and humans as too varied to matter.
disputes · theology-and-religion

One can make robots out of flesh as well as tin, or cover the tin with a fleshy facade. The ways such creatures can be different from humans are varied, extreme, subtle, and irrelevant. One could write a library of novels denoting the difference.

2010-06-30 Dan'l Danehy-Oakes replying to Jeff Wilson #

Argues that a people who create a slave race usurp the Creator's privilege and so act in a daemonic manner, defending the relevance of this point to the Inire thread. Responds to Jeff Wilson's question about whether 'assemble' is preferred to 'create', answering it by framing the issue as moral rather than terminological.
answers · theology-and-religion · theories: Creating a slave race usurps the Creator

It's relevant because a race that creates a (slave) race is usurping the privilege of the Creator -- is in fact acting in daemonic manner.

2010-06-30 James Wynn replying to Dan'l Danehy-Oakes #

Proposes that the hierodules are robots built by the Heirogrammates, not sacred beings, comparing them to missionaries' water purifiers that grew self-aware and used extra-dimensional technology to spread their own manufacture. Responds to Dan'l Danehy-Oakes, who doubted that the Hierofoos were doing God's work, by offering a mechanical alternative that keeps the Heirogrammates innocent of sin.
proposes · puzzle-solving · theology-and-religion · theories: Hierodules are robots built by the Heirogrammates; Hierodules are self-propagating water purifiers gone self-aware

Another interpretation is that "Heirodules" is a grandiose term for "robots created by the Heiro race". That the Heirogrammates made and used robots is not necessarily sinful. Kindly, devout missionaries employ water purifiers. What if the purifiers eventually became so sophisticated that they were _apparently_ self-aware? What if they were sophisticated enough to use extra-dimensional technology to improvise based on their self-propagation programming to ensure the invention of water purifiers everywhere?

2010-06-30 António Pedro Marques replying to Lee Berman #

Replies to Lee Berman by disputing his claim that gods and other preternatural beings are clearly distinct in most mythologies, suggesting ancient pagans may have confused aliens or fallen angels with gods. Asks whether Gene Wolfe explored this possibility in his writing, and points to David Stockton's use of quotation marks as addressing the capitalization concern.
disputes · theology-and-religion · theories: Ancient pagans confused aliens or fallen angels with gods

Lee Berman wrote (30-06-2010 13:37):

Just what a god (in the polytheist sense) and other preternatural being is may not be easy to define in words, but nonetheless I think the distinction is all too clear. Most mythologies have gods and have other preternatural beings, and they are not confused. That said, a powerful enough being that has or seeks universal (i.e., not limited) domain of something usually ends up being considered a god, no matter its origins.

Whole message, with quotes · original
Lee Berman wrote (30-06-2010 13:37):
> brunians-
>> These guys are not gods. They are angels or fairies or some such.
>
> David stockton did use quotes to address your concern. You are using the
> lower-case g for your "gods". Is this not sufficient?
>
> Is it possible that ancient pagans confused aliens/angels (fallen or
> not) with gods, fairies, monsters etc.? Or, more to the point, is it
> worth considering that Gene Wolfe has explored this possibility in his
> writing?

Just what a god (in the polytheist sense) and other preternatural being is 
may not be easy to define in words, but nonetheless I think the distinction 
is all too clear. Most mythologies have gods and have other preternatural 
beings, and they are not confused. That said, a powerful enough being that 
has or seeks universal (i.e., not limited) domain of something usually ends 
up being considered a god, no matter its origins.

2010-06-30 Jeff Wilson replying to David Stockhoff #

Argues that if Rudesind's painting is a composite, its details could have come from anything, and that pulp SF covers are not necessarily more likely inspirations than real photographs of astronauts. Responds to David Stockhoff's suggestion that Wolfe drew on a Fantasy and Science Fiction cover, noting that Apollo surface photos and Alan Bean's work were already ruled out, and proposes the combined details became a traditional painted convention.
disputes · source-and-allusion · theories: Rudesind's astronaut painting is a traditional composite convention

If it's a composite, it could be a composite of anything, and I think it's a bit of a knock to say the pulp covers are more inspirational than actual pictures of actual spacemen on the moon.

A while back, we eliminated all of the then-recently released archive of Apollo 11 surface photography, as well as all of Alan Bean's work of an age to have been influential on BoTNS. If the paining is intended to be an original painting and not a portraitized photograph, it is likely that the combination of details became traditional at some point when some painter deviated from the strictly historical but was well received nonetheless, like wozzname conflating Cupid with cherubim.

Whole message, with quotes · original
On 6/30/2010 9:24 PM, David Stockhoff wrote:
> It occurred to me that Wolfe may have been inspired by an SF magazine
> cover rather than by a painting. I searched The Magazine of Fantasy and
> Science Fiction and found a similar image:
>
> http://www.philsp.com/data/images/f/fantasy_and_science_fiction_197702.jpg
>
> At least it has the gold faceplate and earth. Maybe Rudesind's painting
> is a composite?

If it's a composite, it could be a composite of anything, and I think 
it's a bit of a knock to say the pulp covers are more inspirational than 
actual pictures of actual spacemen on the moon.

A while back, we eliminated all of the then-recently released archive of 
Apollo 11 surface photography, as well as all of Alan Bean's work of an 
age to have been influential on BoTNS. If the paining is intended to be 
an original  painting and not a portraitized photograph, it is likely 
that the combination of details became traditional at some point when 
some painter deviated from the strictly historical but was well received 
nonetheless, like wozzname conflating Cupid with cherubim.

2010-06-30 David Stockhoff replying to David Stockhoff #

Proposes that Wolfe based the astronaut image in Rudesind's painting on a science fiction magazine cover rather than a fine-art painting, citing a 1977 Magazine of Fantasy and Science Fiction cover with a gold faceplate and Earth. Suggests the painting may be a composite and asks whether other pulp magazines are candidates for the source.
proposes · source-and-allusion · theories: Rudesind's painting is based on an SF magazine cover

It occurred to me that Wolfe may have been inspired by an SF magazine cover rather than by a painting. I searched The Magazine of Fantasy and Science Fiction and found a similar image:

http://www.philsp.com/data/images/f/fantasy_and_science_fiction_197702.jpg

At least it has the gold faceplate and earth. Maybe Rudesind's painting is a composite?

The entire archive can be found here:

http://www.philsp.com/mags/fsf.html

Are there other pulp magazines that are candidates?

2010-06-30 David Stockhoff replying to Adam Thornton #

Argues that the painting of an astronaut in The Fifth Head of Cerberus's cleaned-up image is not a direct copy of an Apollo photograph, and that Wolfe may have drawn on published magazine art instead. Responds to Adam Thornton's note that the painting matches no Apollo image by describing his own search for pre-1980 covers from F&SF and Astounding containing astronaut, faceplate, flag and Earth together.
supports · source-and-allusion · theories: Wolfe drew on post-moon-mission magazine art

Yes, exactly. That's why I went in search of art that wasn't published in the traditional sense, but that Wolfe may have seen. The best candidate was painted after 1980. F&SF had post-moon mission covers in the early 1970s.

But there is also Astounding, and there are others.

It's difficult to find ANY image containing all elements: astronaut, faceplate, flag, and Earth.

Whole message, with quotes · original

2010-06-30 David Stockhoff replying to brunians #

Argues that the idea of a local archon pretending to be the Outsider is required by the meaning of usurping, rejecting a reader's looser reading of Inire's appearances. Defends the point by appeal to the concept itself and compares it to Tolkien.
disputes · allegory-and-typology · theories: Inire is a local archon pretending to be the Outsider

No, the definition of usurping demands it. So does the concept of a local archon pretending to be the Outsider. You can explain it any way you want, but that's the concept.

Think Tolkien.

2010-06-30 James Wynn replying to David Stockhoff #

Proposes that Rudesind's painted astronaut may derive from a famous childhood illustration the poster remembers, showing an astronaut on the moon with Earth over his shoulder and an American flag nearby. Replies to David Stockhoff's report that the painting matches no Apollo photograph he could find, suggesting the source is an illustration rather than a photograph.
proposes · source-and-allusion · theories: Rudesind's astronaut painting derives from a famous illustration

There's a picture, maybe an illustration --a famous one-- that I remember from my childhood.
An astronaut on the moon. The Earth over his shoulder. The American flag behind him or beside him. I thought that was the one when I read the description.

2010-07-01 John Watkins replying to David Stockhoff #

Argues that Tzadkiel, who presents himself as the Outsider's agent, may be lying, and lays out three options: a trustworthy archangel, a deceptive alien controlling humanity, or a sincere but fallible higher being. Grounds the options in Tzadkiel's self-presentation and compares the third to the angel in "The Cock and the Angel".
proposes · theology-and-religion · close-textual-reading · theories: Tzadkiel is a sincere but fallible higher being; Tzadkiel is a deceptive alien controlling humanity

Right.

Although now I will say such a thing. Tzadkiel presents himself not as the Outsider but as His agent. Arguably the most important question of the series is whether Tzadkiel is lying. I think there are in fact three options:

1) Tzadkiel is, like a Christian archangel, more or less in direct contact with the will of the deity. Thus he/she is to be completely trusted and is a more or less incontrovertible moral authority.
2) Tzadkiel is a creepy insectoid alien thing and a liar. The pretentions of religion are a means of controlling the human race.
3) Tzadkiel is a "higher" being in some meaningful sense but remains metaphysically removed from the divine, like the angel in "The Cock and the Angel." His/her belief in the Increate and desire to do its will is sincere, and to that end he/she is just doing the best he/she can, but like the angel in the story Tzadkiel remains fallible and infinitely removed from the mind of the Increate.

I go back and forth between 2) and 3) myself.

2010-07-01 David Stockhoff replying to John Watkins #

Extends John Watkins's question of whether Tzadkiel is lying by arguing that whether Yahweh is lawfully in charge of creation yields four possible variations. Lists them as lawful creation, lawful governance, unlawful usurpation of creation, and unlawful usurpation of creative powers, citing The Book of the Long Sun but setting the question aside as beyond scope.
supports · theology-and-religion · theories: Four variations on Yahweh's lawful creative authority

Exactly.

Similarly, but beyond the scope of this list, is Yahweh lawfully in charge of a creation he created, or not?

2 x 2 = 4 possible variations: lawful creation, lawful governance, unlawful usurpation of creation, unlawful usurpation of creative powers. See TBotLS.

Whole message, with quotes · original
Exactly.

Similarly, but beyond the scope of this list, is Yahweh lawfully in 
charge of a creation he created, or not?

unlawful usurpation of creation, unlawful usurpation of creative powers. 
See TBotLS.

2010-07-01 John Watkins replying to brunians #

Disputes a proposed three-way reading of Tzadkiel's status in the Long Sun books, arguing that the first option, where Tzadkiel speaks as God's direct agent and is wholly trustworthy, sharply separates from the third, where his words are merely well-meant. Responds to brunians's framing by noting that the difference between these readings is a wide gulf in how far Tzadkiel's statements can be trusted.
disputes · theology-and-religion · theories: Tzadkiel is God's direct trustworthy agent; Tzadkiel is well-intentioned but fallible

I don't agree. With 1), everything Tzadkiel says can be taken as the Word of God. With 3), everything Tzadkiel says can be taken as being said with good intention. Great gulf fixed there.

Whole message, with quotes · original
I don't agree.  With 1), everything Tzadkiel says can be taken as the Word
of God.  With 3), everything Tzadkiel says can be taken as being said with
good intention.  Great gulf fixed there.

2010-07-01 David Stockhoff replying to brunians #

Suggests that a point about Inire's appearances would be clearer if stated more strongly, removing the hedge "more or less" and using the words "infallible" and "definitely in direct contact." Responds to brunians's quoted remark that there is no meaningful difference between one and three, which concerns the count of Inire's appearances in the Book of the New Sun.
proposes · close-textual-reading

#1 could be phrased more strongly, deleting "more or less" and using the words "infallible" and "definitely in direct contact." That would be more distinct.

As I understand it.

2010-07-01 John Watkins replying to David Stockhoff #

Explains why the poster avoided sorting angelology into Christian and Gnostic schools, arguing that a consciously created myth can allow any separation between angelic and divine will. Supports this by comparing Tolkien's Valar, who are not in constant contact with Ilúvatar and make errors but do not lie, except Melkor and arguably Aulë.
supports · theology-and-religion

Well, I didn't want to get into various schools of angelology in Christian and Gnostic thought. Obvious there must be *some *possible separation between angelic will and divine will, or fallen angels wouldn't have been possible. It's my understanding that there are various interpretations of what this means even in our world, and in a consciously created myth it could mean anything (Tolkien's Valar, for instance, are not in constant communication with Illuvatar or anything like it and they make various errors of judgment. I don't think, however, that they ever lie or commit acts of intentional evil, except for of course Melkor and arguably Aule on one occasion).

Whole message, with quotes · original
Well, I didn't want to get into various schools of angelology in Christian
and Gnostic thought.  Obvious there must be *some *possible separation
between angelic will and divine will, or fallen angels wouldn't have been
possible.  It's my understanding that there are various interpretations of
what this means even in our world, and in a consciously created myth it
could mean anything (Tolkien's Valar, for instance, are not in constant
communication with Illuvatar or anything like it and they make various
errors of judgment.  I don't think, however, that they ever lie or commit
acts of intentional evil, except for of course Melkor and arguably Aule on
one occasion).

2010-07-01 David Stockhoff replying to John Watkins #

Agrees with John Watkins's point about angelic will versus divine will and extends it by sketching how beings at different distances from the deity might act, one more likely to go astray and another more likely to become jealous or rebellious. Responds to Watkins's quoted remarks on angelology and the Valar in a consciously created myth, offering praise for his argument and building on it.
supports · theology-and-religion

Well argued. Extra points for use of Tolkien.

Under #3, presumably, such a being might have enormous powers but feel just as lost as humans. Under #1, such a being might feel significantly more connected.

One can see how a #3 being might more often go astray, even with good intentions, while a #1 being might get carried away with how close to the deity he sits and become jealous or rebellious, causing far more damage.

Whole message, with quotes · original
Well argued. Extra points for use of Tolkien.

Under #3, presumably, such a being might have enormous powers but feel 
just as lost as humans. Under #1, such a being might feel significantly 
more connected.

One can see how a #3 being might more often go astray, even with good 
intentions, while a #1 being might get carried away with how close to 
the deity he sits and become jealous or rebellious, causing far more damage.

2010-07-01 Josh Geller replying to John Watkins #

Argues that every act is willed by God, so what anyone says is their own if mistaken, but true speech belongs to God, in reply to John Watkins's claim about Tzadkiel's words. Responds to Watkins's distinction between taking Tzadkiel's words as the Word of God and as well-intended, stating a theological position without citing specific passages from the books.
disputes · theology-and-religion

Every resultant act is willed by God.

What anyone says can be in error, and if in error belongs to them.

Insofar as it is true, it belongs to God.

All action belongs to God.

.

Whole message, with quotes · original
Every resultant act is willed by God.

What anyone says can be in error, and if in error belongs to them.

Insofar as it is true, it belongs to God.

All action belongs to God.

.


> I don't agree.  With 1), everything Tzadkiel says can be taken as the Word
> of God.  With 3), everything Tzadkiel says can be taken as being said with
> good intention.  Great gulf fixed there.
>
> On Thu, Jul 1, 2010 at 8:29 AM, <[email]> wrote:
>
>> There is no meaningful difference between one and three.
>>
>> .
>>
>>
>> > Right.
>> >
>> > Although now I will say such a thing.  Tzadkiel presents himself not
>> as
>> > the
>> > Outsider but as His agent.  Arguably the most important question of
>> the
>> > series is whether Tzadkiel is lying.  I think there are in fact three
>> > options:
>> >
>> > 1)  Tzadkiel is, like a Christian archangel, more or less in direct
>> > contact
>> > with the will of the deity.  Thus he/she is to be completely trusted
>> and
>> > is
>> > a more or less incontrovertible moral authority.
>> > 2)  Tzadkiel is a creepy insectoid alien thing and a liar.  The
>> > pretentions
>> > of religion are a means of controlling the human race.
>> > 3)  Tzadkiel is a "higher" being in some meaningful sense but remains
>> > metaphysically removed from the divine, like the angel in "The Cock
>> and
>> > the
>> > Angel."  His/her belief in the Increate and desire to do its will is
>> > sincere, and to that end he/she is just doing the best he/she can, but
>> > like
>> > the angel in the story Tzadkiel remains fallible and infinitely
>> removed
>> > from
>> > the mind of the Increate.
>> >
>> > I go back and forth between 2) and 3) myself.
>>

2010-07-01 John Watkins replying to David Stockhoff #

Argues that Tzadkiel's drowning of Urth and the coming of the New Sun could be read as authorized divine judgment, as genocide, or as a clumsy attempt by fallible powerful beings to enact God's will. Builds on David Stockhoff's three-option framework, favoring the third reading and suggesting a redemptive hidden plot in the second.
proposes · theology-and-religion · myth-and-structure · theories: Tzadkiel's act was divinely authorized judgment; Tzadkiel's act is genocide with redemption possible; Fallible powerful beings enacting divine will

Thanks.

Obviously, the effect of Tzadkiel's actions is the coming of the New Sun and the drowning of Urth. Lots and lots of people die in this, maybe for a redemptive higher meaning and maybe not. Under 1), we can be quite confident that the slaughter was authorized by God, like the Flood or the apocalypse, and therefore moral, at least by the author's lights (the reader's mileage may vary.) Under 2), the massacring of Urth is no less than genocide. Under 3), it may be either--a clumsy attempt by incredibly powerful but fallible beings to enact what they understand to be the divine will.

I think 3) maybe makes for the best story. But with 2), there's the possibility of something else going on behind the scenes--maybe over the course of the next seven books--redeeming the act of evil. That would be a pretty great story.

Whole message, with quotes · original
Thanks.

2010-07-01 Jeff Wilson replying to David Stockhoff #

Argues that the astronaut image in the Rudesind cover is a composite of iconic Apollo photographs, citing the Apollo 11 flag salute and Apollo 8 earthrise views. Responds to David Stockhoff's challenge to name sources beyond Apollo 11 and Alan Bean, and compares the blending to Van Gogh placing Ursa Major over European landscapes.
supports · source-and-allusion · theories: Rudesind astronaut image is a composite of Apollo photos

A composite of iconic images, as you speculated. The Apollo 11 photo of Buzz saluting the flag has the grey regolith and the gold visor as well, but it was earlier Apollo 8 orbital photos that show the earth rising over the lunar horizon that were hugely popularized. Some of the Apollo 11 photos do show the earth in the sky, but not terribly prominently. IIRC, Van Gogh was known to have put Ursa Major above southern-looking European landscapes; no reason some unborn master yet to paint couldn't do the same for Neil and Buzz.

Whole message, with quotes · original
On 7/1/2010 6:55 AM, David Stockhoff wrote:
> Jeff, no one said pulp covers were more inspirational than photos.
>
> But if you eliminate Apollo 11 photos and Alan Bean, what's left?

A composite of iconic images, as you speculated. The Apollo 11 photo of 
Buzz saluting the flag has the grey regolith and the gold visor as well, 
but it was earlier Apollo 8 orbital photos that show the earth rising 
over the lunar horizon that were hugely popularized. Some of the Apollo 
11 photos do show the earth in the sky, but not terribly prominently. 
IIRC, Van Gogh was known to have put Ursa Major above southern-looking 
European landscapes; no reason some unborn master yet to paint couldn't 
do the same for Neil and Buzz.

-- 
Jeff Wilson - [email]
IEEE Student Chapter Blog at
< http://ieeetamut.org >

2010-07-01 Jeff Wilson replying to James Wynn #

Argues that the famous "man on the moon" photo shows no flag raised yet, though Buzz Aldrin's left shoulder carries a prominent flag patch. Replies to James Wynn's memory of a childhood picture with a flag by describing the photo, including Aldrin's gold faceplate reflecting Neil Armstrong with the Earth over Aldrin's shoulder.
answers · close-textual-reading · source-and-allusion

The "man on the moon" shot shows Buzz Aldrin's gold faceplate reflecting Neil Armstrong with the Earth over *his* shoulder, but no flag is up yet, though there is a prominent flag patch on Buzz's left shoulder.

Whole message, with quotes · original
On 6/30/2010 11:56 PM, James Wynn wrote:
>
>> Rudesind's painting does not match any of the actual Apollo images I
>> could find, when I went looking a few years ago.
>
> There's a picture, maybe an illustration --a famous one-- that I
> remember from my childhood.
> An astronaut on the moon. The Earth over his shoulder. The American flag
> behind him or beside him. I thought that was the one when I read the
> description.

The "man on the moon" shot shows Buzz Aldrin's gold faceplate reflecting 
Neil Armstrong with the Earth over *his* shoulder, but no flag is up 
yet, though there is a prominent flag patch on Buzz's left  shoulder.

-- 
Jeff Wilson - [email]
IEEE Student Chapter Blog at
< http://ieeetamut.org >

2010-07-01 Jeff Wilson replying to John Watkins #

Argues that Tzadkiel's engineering of the New Sun and the drowning of Urth can be morally defended, since he sees outcomes from Yesod and could undo harmful changes. Responds to John Watkins's framing of the massacre by conceding Tzadkiel's foresight and arguing that the deaths already happened to long-dead Urthlings, possibly sparing some suffering.
disputes · theology-and-religion · theories: Tzadkiel's slaughter of Urth is authorized by God and moral; Yesodi only rearrange deaths that already happened

Tzad cops to #3 although he has the luxury not only of seeing how the machinaitons turn out from his perch in Yesod, but of potentially being able to undo any changes that turn out to suck more rather than less. And arguably, any deaths the Yesodis "cause" have already happened to long-dead Urthlings and they're just rearranging the details, or even saving some from having to suffer in that vale of tears to begin with.

Whole message, with quotes · original
On 7/1/2010 8:24 AM, John Watkins wrote:
> Thanks.
> Obviously, the effect of Tzadkiel's actions is the coming of the New Sun
> and the drowning of Urth.  Lots and lots of people die in this, maybe
> for a redemptive higher meaning and maybe not.  Under 1), we can be
> quite confident that the slaughter was authorized by God, like the Flood
> or the apocalypse, and therefore moral, at least by the author's lights
> (the reader's mileage may vary.)  Under 2), the massacring of Urth is no
> less than genocide.  Under 3), it may be either--a clumsy attempt by
> incredibly powerful but fallible beings to enact what they understand to
> be the divine will.
> I think 3) maybe makes for the best story.  But with 2), there's the
> possibility of something else going on behind the scenes--maybe over the
> course of the next seven books--redeeming the act of evil.  That would
> be a pretty great story.

Tzad cops to #3 although he has the luxury not only of seeing how the

2010-07-01 Jerry Friedman replying to António Pedro Marques #

Argues that a theodicy in which an apparently evil power acts with good intentions is no worse than real-world Christian theodicy, citing dictatorships and the Inquisition. Responds to António Pedro Marques's rejection of such an option, and extends the comparison to The Wizard Knight, where Lothur and Tzadkiel are the figures in question.
disputes · theology-and-religion · theories: Tzadkiel's acts are done with good intentions

From: Ant?nio Pedro Marques <[email]>

...

sense to create a universe in which:
evil power is not evil at all and is doing God's work
who oppose the evil power are not, generally speaking, better people than the others, and in fact seem

Hasn't this been the situation in many dictatorships, especially theocratic ones? In Europe when and where the Inquisition flourished?

I agree with those who say this theodicy is no worse than the one Christians face in the real world.

And what about /The Wizard Knight/? The Aelf could be toys in the hands of evil humans if the humans realized it, just as humans sometimes are for Lothur. (But then the whole non-Christian deism of TWK is strange. Humans seem to be at the bottom instead of in the middle.)

doesn't mean that the acts of Tzadkiel need no atoning for, it just means they're done with
[people with] good intentions is Hell full'.

In English, "The road to Hell is paved with good intentions."

Jerry Friedman
