> John Watkins wrote (01-07-2010 13:18):
>> I think there are in fact three options:
>> 1)  Tzadkiel is, like a Christian archangel, more or less in direct
>> contact with the will of the deity.  Thus he/she is to be completely
>> trusted and is a more or less incontrovertible moral authority.
>> 3)  Tzadkiel is a "higher" being in some meaningful sense but remains
>> metaphysically removed from the divine, like the angel in "The Cock and
>> the Angel."  His/her belief in the Increate and desire to do its will is
>> sincere, and to that end he/she is just doing the best he/she can, but
>> like the angel in the story Tzadkiel remains fallible and infinitely
>> removed from the mind of the Increate.
>
> Afaict 1 and 3 are just points in a continuum. Unless you push 1 further
> and
> make it so that Tzadkiel has no will at all but God's will. In that case
> Tzadkiel would not be a free agent but merely an extension of God. Not
> exactly the same as an angel. I don't think Tzadkiel is such an extension,
> so between 1 and 3 I think only 3, which is really more or less the same
> as
> the lesser sense of 1, works. It is just open to debate how fallible
> Tzadkiel is.
>
> The Valar try not to commit evil because they're good, not because they're
> bound to Eru's will.
>
>  > 2)  Tzadkiel is a creepy insectoid alien thing and a liar.  The
>  > pretentions of religion are a means of controlling the human race.
>  > I go back and forth between 2) and 3) myself.
>
> Option 2 I think is untenable.
>
> - If God is good, then it makes no sense to create a universe in which:
>    + God's creatures are toys in an evil power's hands
>    + God's creatures believe that the evil power is not evil at all and is
> doing God's work
>    + those who oppose the evil power are not, generally speaking, better
> people than the others, and in fact seem to be worse.
>
> - If God isn't good then by what standard can we call such an unfathomable
> being as Tzadkiel evil?
>
> Keeping option 3 doesn't mean that the acts of Tzadkiel need no atoning
> for,
> it just means they're done with the best of intentions and knowledge. We
> have a saying here, 'Of [people with] good intentions is Hell full'.

2010-07-01 John Watkins replying to António Pedro Marques #

Argues that natural disasters like hurricanes and plague raise a harder theodicy problem than the Hierogrammates under Scenario 2, monstrous demigods that human free will might create. Disputes Antonio Pedro Marques's claim by doubting Wolfe would see such beings as inconsistent with a traditional God, then says the thread has drifted from its original question.
disputes · theology-and-religion · author-and-biography · theories: Hierogrammates are monstrous demigods created by human free will

2010/7/1 Ant?nio Pedro Marques <[email]>

Is it? I dunno. I think the existence of, say, hurricanes, or the bubonic plague, is potentially more troubling from a theodicy standpoint than the possibility that the free will of hman beings might create monstrous demigods as the Hierogrammates under Scenario 2 are. Anyway I rather doubt that the author would agree that the existence of such beings is inconsistent with the existence of a traditionally-understood God.

Regardless, we are getting afield of the question.

2010-07-01 Roy C. Lackey replying to John Watkins #

Argues that the Hierogrammates' treatment of humans repeats the cruelty the Hieros once used to shape them, and that this gives them a rationale for their behaviour. Supports John Watkins by quoting Severian's account in chapter XXXIV, "The Key to the Universe," of The Citadel of the Autarch, and by noting Peter Wright's doubts about the Hierogrammates.
supports · close-textual-reading · theories: Hierogrammates repay humans by shaping them as they were shaped

This is probably a futile attempt to drag this discussion back to what Wolfe actually wrote, but I am going to do it anyway.

What has gotten lost is the truth of what Dan'l and John Watkins alluded to. I invite anyone who doesn't understand or doesn't remember the "The Key to the Universe" chapter (XXXIV) of CITADEL to read it again. (There is more on the topic in URTH.) It contains a summary of Severian's understanding.

This is the gist. The human beings of the First Empire formed (that is the verb used) a new race of beings. The new beings were cruelly fashioned from lesser beings (less than humans). The end result was the race we know as the Hierogrammates. The cruel humans who formed them were the Hieros.

That much is straightforward. Here is where the subject turns tricky and leads some to question the motives and moral authority of the Hierogrammates. (Peter Wright comes to mind.)

"However it may be, they [the Hierogrammates] shape us now as they themselves were shaped; it is at once their repayment and their revenge. "The Hierodules they have found too, and formed more quickly, to serve them in this universe."

These are the textual facts, as Severian understood them. The Hierogrammates are doing unto human beings much the same thing that humans had done unto them. They also tinkered with some sort of beings to form their holy slaves.

-Roy

Whole message, with quotes · original

2010-07-01 James Wynn replying to Jerry Friedman #

Argues that The Wizard Knight's hierarchy of beings reflects human degeneracy, with humans enslaved to low instincts and so unable to hold Aelfrice in awe. Responds to Jerry Friedman's remark that humans sit at the bottom of the non-Christian deism, citing the scene where Valfather bows before Michael.
disputes · theology-and-religion · close-textual-reading · theories: Humanity is degenerate at the bottom of TWK's hierarchy; The Wizard Knight is Wolfe's most moralistic novel

I think that was the point. Human degeneracy had caused them to be enslaved by their lowest instincts. Humanity ought to have been seeking to serve the ideals of Valfather and then Michael's and then discover the ideals that are even higher still.

Due to their enslavement to Arnthor (a dragon in disguise from a realm two levels lower), even Aelfrice (representing lower instincts but higher than those that had enslaved humanity) was also enslaved by those instincts and hence rebelled -- because humans had relinquished the ideals necessary to hold the beings of Aelfrice in awe.

This is a picture of a man with his priorities upside down. TWK might well be viewed as the most moralistic novel Wolfe has ever written. The following is a key scene: Valfather bowing before Michael, as Michael tells Abel that to be a knight he must be able to command Disiri with the same authority.

u+16b9

Whole message, with quotes · original

> for Lothur.  (But then the whole non-Christian deism of TWK is strange.  Humans seem to be at the bottom instead of in the middle.)
>

enslaved by their lowest instincts. Humanity ought to have been seeking 
to serve the ideals of Valfather and then Michael's and then discover

2010-07-01 António Pedro Marques replying to John Watkins #

Argues that Tzadkiel's position on the continuum between complete divine trust and metaphysical distance is best read as option 3, with his acts done in good intentions yet still needing atonement. Responds to John Watkins by rejecting option 2 as untenable, reasoning that a good God would not create a world where evil powers manipulate creatures who wrongly think them godly.
disputes · theology-and-religion · theories: Tzadkiel is a higher being removed from the divine; Tzadkiel's acts need atonement despite good intentions

John Watkins wrote (01-07-2010 13:18):

Afaict 1 and 3 are just points in a continuum. Unless you push 1 further and make it so that Tzadkiel has no will at all but God's will. In that case Tzadkiel would not be a free agent but merely an extension of God. Not exactly the same as an angel. I don't think Tzadkiel is such an extension, so between 1 and 3 I think only 3, which is really more or less the same as the lesser sense of 1, works. It is just open to debate how fallible Tzadkiel is.

The Valar try not to commit evil because they're good, not because they're bound to Eru's will.

Option 2 I think is untenable.

- If God is good, then it makes no sense to create a universe in which: + God's creatures are toys in an evil power's hands
+ God's creatures believe that the evil power is not evil at all and is doing God's work
+ those who oppose the evil power are not, generally speaking, better people than the others, and in fact seem to be worse.

- If God isn't good then by what standard can we call such an unfathomable being as Tzadkiel evil?

Keeping option 3 doesn't mean that the acts of Tzadkiel need no atoning for, it just means they're done with the best of intentions and knowledge. We have a saying here, 'Of [people with] good intentions is Hell full'.

Antonio

Whole message, with quotes · original
John Watkins wrote (01-07-2010 13:18):
> I think there are in fact three options:
> 1)  Tzadkiel is, like a Christian archangel, more or less in direct
> contact with the will of the deity.  Thus he/she is to be completely
> trusted and is a more or less incontrovertible moral authority.
> 3)  Tzadkiel is a "higher" being in some meaningful sense but remains
> metaphysically removed from the divine, like the angel in "The Cock and
> the Angel."  His/her belief in the Increate and desire to do its will is
> sincere, and to that end he/she is just doing the best he/she can, but
> like the angel in the story Tzadkiel remains fallible and infinitely
> removed from the mind of the Increate.

Afaict 1 and 3 are just points in a continuum. Unless you push 1 further and 
make it so that Tzadkiel has no will at all but God's will. In that case 
Tzadkiel would not be a free agent but merely an extension of God. Not 
exactly the same as an angel. I don't think Tzadkiel is such an extension, 
so between 1 and 3 I think only 3, which is really more or less the same as 
the lesser sense of 1, works. It is just open to debate how fallible 
Tzadkiel is.

The Valar try not to commit evil because they're good, not because they're 
bound to Eru's will.

 > 2)  Tzadkiel is a creepy insectoid alien thing and a liar.  The
 > pretentions of religion are a means of controlling the human race.
 > I go back and forth between 2) and 3) myself.

Option 2 I think is untenable.

- If God is good, then it makes no sense to create a universe in which:
   + God's creatures are toys in an evil power's hands
   + God's creatures believe that the evil power is not evil at all and is 
doing God's work
   + those who oppose the evil power are not, generally speaking, better 
people than the others, and in fact seem to be worse.

- If God isn't good then by what standard can we call such an unfathomable 
being as Tzadkiel evil?

Keeping option 3 doesn't mean that the acts of Tzadkiel need no atoning for, 
it just means they're done with the best of intentions and knowledge. We 
have a saying here, 'Of [people with] good intentions is Hell full'.

Antonio

2010-07-01 John Watkins replying to António Pedro Marques #

Explains a gnostic cosmology in which a debased demiurge creates the world while a benign true God exists, and how Gnostic Christianity casts Jesus as a messenger rejecting the demiurge. Responds to António Pedro Marques by citing orthodox 'lord of this world' language and C.S. Lewis's Space Trilogy, where Satan rules Earth under the true God.
answers · theology-and-religion · theories: Satan as rebellious planetary ruler under God

2010/7/1 Ant?nio Pedro Marques <[email]>

Yes. Gnosticism posits that "the world" is the creation of the demiurge, a debased being posing as the true God, but that a benign God still exists and calls for our loyalty. Gnostic Christianity (or Gnosticism proper) posits that Jesus Christ was a sort of messenger from the true God, preaching a rejection of the demiurge. Orthodox Christianity refers at times to the Devil as "the lord of [the/this] world," with much the same significance.

See C.S. Lewis's Space Trilogy for a literalization of this kind of cosmology. There, Satan is the local ruler--the planetary genius of Earth--and the true God is the ruler of the universe, from whom Satan originally had authority in fiefdom, but against whom Satan has rebelled. Of course, the scale is different from the 2) hypothetical (planet versus universe), but from the perspective of Earth there's very little different.

Whole message, with quotes · original
2010/7/1 Ant?nio Pedro Marques <[email]>

>

Yes.  Gnosticism posits that "the world" is the creation of the demiurge, a
debased being posing as the true God, but that a benign God still exists and
calls for our loyalty.  Gnostic Christianity (or Gnosticism proper) posits
that Jesus Christ was a sort of messenger from the true God, preaching a
rejection of the demiurge.  Orthodox Christianity refers at times to the
Devil as "the lord of [the/this] world," with much the same significance.

See C.S. Lewis's Space Trilogy for a literalization of this kind of
cosmology.  There, Satan is the local ruler--the planetary genius of
Earth--and the true God is the ruler of the universe, from whom Satan
originally had authority in fiefdom, but against whom Satan has rebelled.
Of course, the scale is different from the 2) hypothetical (planet versus
universe), but from the perspective of Earth there's very little different.

2010-07-01 James Wynn replying to John Watkins #

Argues that Lewis's cosmology differs from Gnosticism: in Gnostic systems the demiurge is the genuine but imperfect Creator, while in Lewis's Space Trilogy Satan is a local administrator in revolt. Adds an aside on That Hideous Strength, where the Earth-facing moon is wasted from a war while the unseen far side is idyllic Loyalist forest.
disputes · theology-and-religion

At risk of seeming pedantic, Lewis' cosmology is a little different from the Gnostic version. In the Gnostic version, the demiurge (or just as often, the demiurges) is the genuine Creator. But because he is imperfect, --not the Increate-- the world is imperfect.

In Lewis' version, the Satan is, as you say, a local administrator in revolt.

Incidentally, I really like the idea in That Hideous Strength in which the Earth-facing side of the moon was blasted into a wasteland during the war between the Revolutionaries and the Loyalists, but the "Dark Side" (which at the time of writing had never been seen) was covered in idyllic forests, and was in Loyalist territory.

u+16b9

2010-07-01 John Watkins replying to António Pedro Marques #

Disputes António Pedro Marques's claim that Gnostics do not identify the demiurge with God, arguing instead that Gnostics take the demiurge to be the God of the Old Testament. Contrasts this with the Christian view that pagan gods are devils, so the parallel between the two positions does not fail as stated.
disputes · theology-and-religion

This is mistaken. The Gnostic posits that the demiurge is in fact the God of the Old Testament; while it's at least consistent with Christianity to posit that the gods worshipped by various pagans are identifiable with devils.

Whole message, with quotes · original
This is mistaken. The Gnostic posits that the demiurge is in fact the
God of the Old Testament; while it's at least consistent with
Christianity to posit that the gods worshipped by various pagans are
identifiable with devils.

2010-07-01 John Watkins replying to António Pedro Marques #

Argues that Pas is evil while his servants, such as Silk, can still be good, and that lesser servants like Incus improve through greater service. Responds to Antonio Pedro Marques's point that a wholly evil Tzadkiel would make his opponents morally dubious, citing the view that good service even to a false god is sanctifying.
disputes · theology-and-religion · allegory-and-typology · theories: Good service to a false god is sanctifying

I"m confused by this too.

Certainly Pas is evil. Certainly many of his servants (Silk) are good. And of those servants who aren't very good, they tend to be improved by greater service (Incus). Wolfe seems to believe and in fact very nearly states in canon that good service to even a false god is sanctifying.

2010-07-01 António Pedro Marques replying to Jerry Friedman #

Disputes the view that the Inquisition or dictatorships make the books' theodicy comparable to real-world evil, arguing that domination of the whole Universe differs in scale and that evil seldom wins as perceived good. Responds to António Pedro Marques, noting he has not yet read The Wizard Knight and has not replied on Agia, and citing Stockholm syndrome as only a limited case.
disputes · theology-and-religion · theories: Evil rarely establishes itself as moral referent

Jerry Friedman wrote (01-07-2010 20:06):

I don't think so. I think it's very different if nothing else in terms of scale. You can hardly say the Inquisition had domain over the Universe, and whatever power dictatorships have is transient. Granted, sometimes evil can establish itself as the moral referent - cf. 'Stockholm's syndrome' - but so far, that I've seen, only in limited scale.

If the real world saw triumph of Evil perceived as Good in most parts and for most of the time, I'd agree. But I don't see the world that way.

Here I should disclose that I haven't read TWK yet.

Somehow I never remember that.

Still haven't had the time to answer to your reply on Agia.

2010-07-01 Jeff Wilson replying to David Stockhoff #

Argues that the Yesodis act from a vantage above or outside the chronology of Urth's universe rather than from its far end, and that Yesod does not follow the Earth-Urth-Ushas sequence. Grounds this in the idea that Yesodis help a parallel universe they have no stake in, and weighs a cynical experimental reading against a benevolent one, favoring the benevolent reading.
proposes · theology-and-religion · chronology-and-plot-logic · theories: Yesodis intervene from above the universe's chronology; Yesodis help a parallel universe benevolently

Many such, though the perspective might be more from above than from the chronological end. Yesod doesn't seem to follow Earth-Urth-Ushas in time. In a a way, that would support the beneficence of the Yesodis, who in that case are helping this parallel or offshoot universe they have no stake in to avoid the sort of horrid durance that drove their forebears off of or out of the cycle of bangs and gnabs before they perfected their union with the Increate.

This can still be read cynically as the Yesodis experimenting on a serially unconnected universe so that they don't snuff it if they accidentally break the chain of causality by killing *everyone*, but I like the tough love, Clarkean version better.

Whole message, with quotes · original

2010-07-01 David Stockhoff replying to Jeff Wilson #

Proposes that the painting of an astronaut in The Fifth Head of Cerberus may be a work by an invented painter, in Borgesian fashion, rather than something Wolfe invented directly. Builds on Jeff Wilson's earlier point about composite iconic images, which the post's quoted context describes through Apollo photos and Van Gogh.
proposes · source-and-allusion · theories: The astronaut painting is by an invented painter

That's true. So perhaps this painting is not something Wolfe made up, but rather a painting by a person he made up, in Borgesian fashion.

Either way, it is a study in symbols/icons becoming more real than reality.

Unless, of course, there IS a real model.

Whole message, with quotes · original

> to paint couldn't do the same for Neil and Buzz.

2010-07-01 Gerry Quinn replying to John Watkins #

Argues that the proverb about good intentions and hell does not hold in Wolfe's world, citing Lucifer's denial in the short story 'The Last Thrilling Wonder Story'. Quotes Lucifer's line that the pavements are not laid of good intentions and adds that he may be lying, since he does that.
disputes · close-textual-reading · theories: Lucifer's denial of good intentions is unreliable

2010/7/1 Ant?nio Pedro Marques <[email]>

Keeping option 3 doesn't mean that the acts of Tzadkiel need no atoning for, it just means they're done with the best of intentions and knowledge. We have a saying here, 'Of [people with] good intentions is Hell full'. A common saying in English is "the road to Hell is paved with good intentions".

However, In Wolfe's short 'The Last Thrilling Wonder Story', this is denied by one who should know:

Luciifer smiled at it. "That will not break my pavements," he said. "Despite so many wild rumours, they are not laid of good intentiona - which are, in my experience, quite easily broken".

Of course he could be lying. He does that.

- Gerry Quinn

Whole message, with quotes · original
  2010/7/1 Ant?nio Pedro Marques <[email]>

2010-07-01 David Stockhoff replying to brunians #

Argues that the astronaut image in the linked photo is the same picture as one in Rudesind's gallery, or a painting or printout of it. Supports this by claiming Wolfe builds his images from existing materials rather than inventing composites, responding to brunians' remarks about pedantry.
proposes · source-and-allusion · theories: The astronaut image is a real photo or print from Rudesind's gallery

I was referring to the photo in the link. I think that's the photo in Rudesind's gallery, or a painting of it, or a printout on really tough paper and ink.

The reason I believe this is that Wolfe does not make things up when he can construct them out of existing materials. No presumed composite would satisfy him.

Jeff Wilson wrote:

>> So you think the Yesodis are operating a time-travel
>> historical-intervention scheme, from a perspective at the end of Urth's
>> universe?
>
> Many such, though the perspective might be more from above than from 
> the chronological end. Yesod doesn't seem to follow Earth-Urth-Ushas 
> in time. In a a way, that would support the beneficence of the 
> Yesodis, who in that case are helping this parallel or offshoot 
> universe they have no stake in to avoid the sort of horrid durance 
> that drove their forebears off of or out of the cycle of bangs and 
> gnabs before they perfected their union with the Increate.
>
> This can still be read cynically as the Yesodis experimenting on a 
> serially unconnected universe so that they don't snuff it if they 
> accidentally break the chain of causality by killing *everyone*, but I 
> like the tough love, Clarkean version better.
>

2010-07-01 Jeff Wilson replying to David Stockhoff #

Argues that the photograph linked in the thread does not match the picture Rudesind cleans in The Urth of the New Sun, citing the passage's details. Compares the description of the armored figure, staff, golden visor and blue Earth over the shoulder against the linked autographed photo, which shows no staff and a different view.
disputes · close-textual-reading

I do not think it matches the passage. To refresh:

"The picture he was cleaning showed an armored figure standing in a desolate landscape. It had no weapon, but held a staff bearing a strange, stiff banner. The visor of this figure's helmet was entirely of gold, without eye slits or ventilation; in its polished surface the deathly desert could be seen in reflection, and nothing more."

and

"'...Today I took the notion to clean it again. And it needs it?see how nice it's brightening up? There's your blue Urth coming over his shoulder again, fresh as the Autarch's fish.'"

and

"...'Is that the moon? I have been told it's more fertile.' "'Now it is, yes. This was done before they got it irrigated. See that gray-brown? In those times, that's what you'd see if you looked up at her. Not green like she is now.'"

The linked, autographed photo does not show a staff, and the visor pointedly shows the flag and the sun more than the moonscape. The earth is above his head rather than either shoulder.

Whole message, with quotes · original
On 7/1/2010 9:47 PM, David Stockhoff wrote:
> I was referring to the photo in the link. I think that's the photo in
> Rudesind's gallery, or a painting of it, or a printout on really tough
> paper and ink.

I do not think it matches the passage. To refresh:

"The picture he was cleaning showed an armored figure standing in a
desolate landscape. It had no weapon, but held a staff bearing a
strange, stiff banner. The visor of this figure's helmet was entirely of
gold, without eye slits or ventilation; in its polished surface the
deathly desert could be seen in reflection, and nothing more."

and

"'...Today I took the notion to clean it again. And it needs it?see how
nice it's brightening up? There's your blue Urth coming over his
shoulder again, fresh as the Autarch's fish.'"

and

"...'Is that the moon? I have been told it's more fertile.'
"'Now it is, yes. This was done before they got it irrigated. See that
gray-brown? In those times, that's what you'd see if you looked up at
her. Not green like she is now.'"


The linked, autographed photo does not show a staff, and the visor 
pointedly shows the flag and the sun more than the moonscape. The earth 
is above his head rather than either shoulder.

2010-07-01 António Pedro Marques replying to John Watkins #

Disputes John Watkins's claim that a universe ruled by an evil being posing as God is a Gnostic premise comparable to Christianity, arguing that Gnostics treat the demiurge as antagonistic to God rather than as God. Responds by distinguishing the demiurge from the true God and noting that worshippers of God are not worshipping the demiurge, so the parallel fails.
disputes · theology-and-religion

But what they consider is that the demiurge is antagonistic to God. They do not consider the demiurge to be God, and those who they see worshipping God are not worshipping what they consider as the demiurge. So I think the parallel fails.

Whole message, with quotes · original
John Watkins wrote:
>
>
> 2010/7/1 Ant?nio Pedro Marques <[email]
> <mailto:[email]>>
>
>     John Watkins wrote (01-07-2010 19:09):
>
>         2010/7/1 Ant?nio Pedro Marques <[email]
>         <mailto:[email]>
>             John Watkins wrote (01-07-2010 18:22):
>
>             A universe under the dominion of an evil being that poses and is
>             seen as doing God's will is consistent with the existence of a
>             benign God?
>
>         It's the premise of Gnosticism, isn't it?  And arguably of
>         Christianity,
>         just on a grander scale.
>
>
>     Could you elaborate?
>
> Yes.  Gnosticism posits that "the world" is the creation of the
> demiurge, a debased being posing as the true God, but that a benign God
> still exists and calls for our loyalty.  Gnostic Christianity (or
> Gnosticism proper) posits that Jesus Christ was a sort of messenger from
> the true God, preaching a rejection of the demiurge.  Orthodox
> Christianity refers at times to the Devil as "the lord of [the/this]
> world," with much the same significance.

But what they consider is that the demiurge is antagonistic to God. They do 
not consider the demiurge to be God, and those who they see worshipping God 
are not worshipping what they consider as the demiurge. So I think the 
parallel fails.

> See C.S. Lewis's Space Trilogy for a literalization of this kind of
> cosmology.  There, Satan is the local ruler--the planetary genius of
> Earth--and the true God is the ruler of the universe, from whom Satan
> originally had authority in fiefdom, but against whom Satan has
> rebelled.  Of course, the scale is different from the 2) hypothetical
> (planet versus universe), but from the perspective of Earth there's very
> little different.
>

2010-07-02 António Pedro Marques replying to António Pedro Marques #

Argues that the gnostic parallel for The Book of the New Sun fails only in a version where an evil Tzadkiel controls humanity, since in that version his servants would still be good and his opponents dubious. Grounds this in the text as remembered, setting aside characters who fought merely to survive, and in a reading of option 2 that makes Tzadkiel evil.
disputes · theology-and-religion · close-textual-reading · theories: Tzadkiel as evil demiurge fails gnostic parallel

I mean, it's not the parallel between BNS and gnostic christianity that fails, it's the parallel to a BNS in which an evil Tazdkiel had wide moral control over mankind, such that those who opposed him were of dubious moral stature, that does. For such is the option offered by 2 - it hypothesizes that Tzadkiel is evil (let's not go to Machen's The White People's prologue), but it doesn't remove the fact that those who serve and revere him tend to be good, whereas those who oppose him tend a bit to the thug side - at least if I remember the text (put aside the ones who were merely fighting for their lives).

Whole message, with quotes · original

2010-07-02 Ryan Dunn replying to brunians #

Argues that the astronaut image in The Book of the New Sun is a valuable clue revealing the far-future setting is Earth, and says the poster enjoyed searching for the picture online. Responds to Josh's suggestion that the astronaut discussion is pedantic by comparing it to the earlier Agia-as-robot debate and mentioning the gnostic and angelic talk about Zak that the poster finds hard to follow.
supports · close-textual-reading · theories: the astronaut image reveals the far-future planet is Earth

Josh,

Do you really think this is any more pedantic than our Agia as robot discourse?

I happened to find my virgin reading of that passage (about the astronaut) one of the first OH WOW! moments of the series. Because it was more clear than the propulsion chamber clue in revealing that we are in a distant future, but that the planet is OUR planet, Earth.

I've, on more than one occasion, tried to find this image online. It's pretty fun to hunt for it. I don't think it's pedantic, however.

The talk of gnostic gods, and Zak's role as archangel or insectoid, and so forth really hurts my brain, though.

:)

...ryan

2010-07-02 Ryan Dunn replying to David Stockhoff #

Identifies the astronaut photo behind the image of the warrior in The Shadow of the Torturer as an Apollo 17 picture of Harrison Schmitt, and links higher-resolution versions. Notes that the photo differs from Wolfe's description because Wolfe says the figure holds a staff, so it is not the exact source.
informs · source-and-allusion · close-textual-reading

Yeah, this is a better version of that image, hi-res:

http://history.nasa.gov/alsj/a17/AS17-134-20387HR.jpg

It's not the exact image from SotT, because Wolfe says the warrior is holding a staff.

The image is from the Apollo 17 mission, and the astronaut is Harrison "Jack" Schmitt, who has had a few quite awesome photos taken of him:

http://1.bp.blogspot.com/_O_L1W8HmE8E/S8AIwQXNFVI/AAAAAAAABdE/jiK9msGVSlc/s1600/Jack+Schmitt.jpg

http://apod.nasa.gov/apod/image/0012/lunarscape_apollo17_big.jpg

http://4.bp.blogspot.com/_AsL5s6RwTrk/Sc4F10aUY6I/AAAAAAAAB2s/LB-IOJ8LeEk/s1600/as17-140-21391.jpg

http://hubblesite.org/explore_astronomy/skywatch/db/263/images/large.jpg

http://www.airportjournals.com/Photos/0509/X/0509011_9.jpg

http://images.google.com/hosted/life/f?q=harrison+jack+schmitt&prev=/images%3Fq%3Dharrison%2Bjack%2Bschmitt%26start%3D21%26um%3D1%26hl%3Den%26client%3Dsafari%26sa%3DX%26rls%3Den%26ndsp%3D21%26tbs%3Disch:1,isz:l&imgurl=245970a4d79b352c

http://3.bp.blogspot.com/_04kZGR_ltmE/SskHoHLHHtI/AAAAAAAAExw/JWtP2ctZzoM/s1600/as17-134-20382.jpg

...ryan

2010-07-02 António Pedro Marques replying to John Watkins #

Argues that pagan gods Christians call devils were often worshipped out of fear rather than considered good, so worship does not imply goodness. Responds to John Watkins's claim about the Gnostic demiurge and the Old Testament God by noting that the Jewish God's wrath and jealousy are not benign traits and that fallen angels need not be devils.
disputes · theology-and-religion

Only the jews, a very small part of the world's population, worshipped the God of the OT as such, and they certainly went to end talking about his wrath, jealousy and favouritisms, which aren't exactly benign traits.

Angelic beings, fallen or not, not necessarily devils.
But how many pagan gods considered evil by christians were not considered evil by their own worshippers? Many deities were worshipped out of fear. It's one thing to give worship to, other to consider good. To worship and consider good something which actually is evil raises the bar quite a bit.

Whole message, with quotes · original
John Watkins wrote:
> This is mistaken. The Gnostic posits that the demiurge is in fact the
> God of the Old Testament;

Only the jews, a very small part of the world's population, worshipped the 
God of the OT as such, and they certainly went to end talking about his 
wrath, jealousy and favouritisms, which aren't exactly benign traits.

> while it's at least consistent with
> Christianity to posit that the gods worshipped by various pagans are
> identifiable with devils.

Angelic beings, fallen or not, not necessarily devils.
But how many pagan gods considered evil by christians were not considered 
evil by their own worshippers? Many deities were worshipped out of fear. 
It's one thing to give worship to, other to consider good.
To worship and consider good something which actually is evil raises the bar 
quite a bit.

Asks where the poster should draw the line on pedantry, comparing tracing the source of the astronaut painting with locating a doll that escaped a lemonwood box. questions

Not a jibe, mind you, an honest curiosity.

...ryan

2010-07-02 António Pedro Marques replying to John Watkins #

Argues that Tzadkiel cannot be an evil god in the books, since an evil master would make his servants morally superior to his opponents, and that the Whorl's moral goodness comes from community reshaping rather than from its gods. Responds to John Watkins by denying that Silk considers Pas good and that Auk or Incus improve because of Pas's evil.
disputes · theology-and-religion · close-textual-reading · theories: Tzadkiel is not an evil god; Whorl morals come from community reshaping, not gods

I'm confused as to why you don't write your remarks below the part of the message whcih they address.

But I didn't say evil masters can't have good servants. I said that in the books, if Tzadkiel were evil, you'd have an evil master whose servants would be better than his opponents - i.e., those who oppose the evil master are of a quite lesser moral standing than those who serve him - and would actually consider the master to be good. I don't recall Silk ever considering Pas good, nor actually any of the Whorl gods. In fact, from what I recall, the people in the Whorl are fed up with their gods.

Quite sure, inasmuch as the false (here the issue is 'evil', not 'false') or evil god is also ultimately bound to God's plan for the world. It's not because of Pas's evilness that Auk or Incus improve (I'm not even certain their Incus shows much of an improvement, but that's another matter).

What salvific elements are there in the Whorl's religion are due either to the community's appropriation and improvement by serious reshaping (motivated by individual episodes of englightenment or not) of the basics they were primed with, or whatever of the originals shows through the transvestite. It's not from Scylla's demand of 50 children that the good people of the Whorl got their morals.

Whole message, with quotes · original

2010-07-02 James Wynn replying to Ryan Dunn #

Argues that the uncropped NASA photograph works as the picture Severian cleans in The Shadow of the Torturer, since the staff and banner are visible. Supports the point by noting that the moon landing photos were also cropped, answering the conspiracy claim of perfect composition, and quoting the passage describing the armored figure holding a staff.
supports · close-textual-reading · source-and-allusion · theories: The cleaned picture is the uncropped Apollo 17 photograph

The uncropped version of this picture works fine I think. It would show the flag on it's pole.

One of the arguments from the conspiracists that the moon landings were faked is that their composition is perfect despite their cameras having no viewfinders. But the truth is that all the pictures were cropped.

Full quote:
"The picture he was cleaning showed an armored figure standing in a desolate landscape. It had no weapon, but held a staff bearing a strange, stiff banner.
The visor of this figure's helmet was entirely of gold, without eye slits or ventilation; in its polished surface the deathly desert could be seen in reflection, and nothing more. [snip] 'There's your blue Urth coming over his shoulder again, fresh as the Autarch's fish.' "

2010-07-02 Gerry Quinn replying to John Watkins #

Argues that Pas, creator of the Whorl, cannot be called evil, since evil means opposition to good rather than mere imperfection, and Typhon is not Pas. Responds to John Watkins' claim that Pas is evil by citing the Whorl's good, Silk's remarks in The Book of the Long Sun about its evil, and Pas's care for it.
disputes · theology-and-religion · theories: Pas is not evil

From: "John Watkins" <[email]>
And of those servants who aren't very good, they tend to be improved by greater service (Incus). Wolfe seems to believe and in fact very nearly states in canon that good service to even a false god is sanctifying.

I'm not sure the concept 'Pas is evil' makes much real sense (we can posit that Typhon was evil, but Typhon is not exactly Pas).

Pas is the creator of the Whorl, which has much good in it, even if, as Silk animadverts towards the end of _TBotLS_ it also has much evil. And he seems to be doing his best for it, in his way.

Evil is generally defined as being opposite to good, not as mere imperfection. The imperfect has evil within it, but it cannot reasonably be cosidered synonymous with evil.

- Gerry Quinn

Whole message, with quotes · original
From: "John Watkins" <[email]>
> Certainly Pas is evil. Certainly many of his servants (Silk) are good.
And of those servants who aren't very good, they tend to be improved
by greater service (Incus). Wolfe seems to believe and in fact very
nearly states in canon that good service to even a false god is
sanctifying.

I'm not sure the concept 'Pas is evil' makes much real sense (we can posit 
that Typhon was evil, but Typhon is not exactly Pas).

Pas is the creator of the Whorl, which has much good in it, even if, as Silk 
animadverts towards the end of _TBotLS_ it also has much evil.  And he seems 
to be doing his best for it, in his way.

Evil is generally defined as being opposite to good, not as mere 
imperfection.  The imperfect has evil within it, but it cannot reasonably be 
cosidered synonymous with evil.

- Gerry Quinn

2010-07-02 Jeff Wilson replying to António Pedro Marques #

Reports pixel measurements of an astronaut image, locating the Earth and the helmet horizontally and noting that after rotating the picture to align local vertical, the planet sits almost directly above the helmet. 2010-07-02 Dave Lebling replying to António Pedro Marques #

Argues that Wolfe's description of the astronaut's visor image in The Shadow of the Torturer was drawn from memory of Apollo photos and paintings rather than a specific photograph, so the picture need not match a real one. Responds to the nitpicking over the image's placement and the reflection on the visor, noting that Wolfe likely took authorial liberties and that the poster's own mental picture now follows Wolfe's text.
disputes · source-and-allusion · close-textual-reading · theories: Wolfe's astronaut image is based on memory rather than a specific photograph

I'm really fairly amazed at the level of nit-pickery over this question. Let us cast our minds back to the thrilling days of yesteryear (the late 70s), when Wolfe wrote SotT. There was no internet on which you could look up all the thousands of then-unreleased Apollo program pictures, there were a few places you could find pictures in hardcopy (Life -- already moribund, National Geographic, a few coffee table books, etc.). As an SF fan, I'm sure he had resources such as the F&SF cover -- or not, he may not be a pack rat.

It seems quite obvious that the description he wrote is based on his memories of various photos and paintings he had actually seen, but he very likely wasn't staring at an actual photo when he wrote the words. Even if he was, who's to say he might not have taken auctorial liberties with what he saw?

I know that after many re-readings of SotT, my minds-eye image of the "canonical" Apollo photograph is now the (non-existent) one Wolfe described.

-- David Lebling, aka vizcacha

2010/7/2 Ant?nio Pedro Marques <[email]>

2010-07-02 David Stockhoff replying to Dave Lebling #

Argues that the astronaut image in Shadow of the Torturer may be a memory, a painting or an SF in-joke rather than a photograph Wolfe saw, and that finding its source still matters for interpreting the novel. Responds to Dave Lebling's impatience with nitpicking by listing reasons the question is worthwhile, including memory, myth, Borgesian artifice and the image being the book's only plain link to the 20th century.
proposes · source-and-allusion · close-textual-reading · theories: Astronaut image is a deliberate false insertion

You have a point about finding specific photos. Even though Wolfe was in publishing and no doubt knows more than a little about how and where to find images, there was no Web gallery to browse. However, we're not necessarily looking for a photo Wolfe saw. It could have been a photo someone else saw and painted. It could have been an image in our national memory, or our shared fannish memory. It could have been his own bad memory.

I still think this is an worthwhile quest, because:

(1) The book may have had the same effect on me as it did on you, and I'd be fascinated to know the truth.

(2) Issues of memory are hardly irrelevant to the novel. Nor are issues of myth.

(3) Is the painting a Borgesian artefact? Is it a knowingly false but truer-than-true insertion?

(4) Is it an SF in-joke, as might be if the image was on the cover of Astounding?

Error? Red herring? MacGuffin? These are basic interpretive questions. I've already suggested that I don't think Wolfe makes things up when he has an alternative at hand. Thus the question: was there an alternative to making it up?

Keep in mind that this painting is the only plain link to the 20th c in the entire book(s), if you set aside the many pulp references.

2010-07-02 Adam Thornton replying to António Pedro Marques #

Points out that the astronauts' visors carry a gold coating, citing a NASA page that describes the inner surface of the polysulfone sun visor as gold-coated for light protection and heat reduction. Responds to António Pedro Marques, who had suggested the visor might be golden in the photograph; the post supplies the documented fact that settles the point.
informs · source-and-allusion

On Fri, 02 Jul 2010 18:00:11 +0100, Ant?nio Pedro Marques

Well, er....

http://history.nasa.gov/alsj/alsj-LEVA.html

"The inner surface of the polysulfone sun visor has a gold coating which provides protection against light and reduces heat gain within the helmet."

Well, er....

2010-07-02 Ryan Dunn replying to Dave Lebling #

Argues that Wolfe could have found the Apollo photographs he used in The Shadow of the Torturer through archival research, and suggests the pinakotheken image was a composed wide shot of an astronaut with a flag and rising Earth. Responds to Dave Lebling's claim that the 1970s lacked access to such pictures, pointing to microfiche research, print releases and autographed prints as evidence.
disputes · source-and-allusion · theories: Wolfe composed the astronaut image from iconic Apollo photographs

David,

Wolfe also built an entire SF series with hundreds of extinct words which he found through diligent research. I wouldn't put hours spent at the microfiche table past him, and those NASA photos would have been released I'm almost certain. Hell, most of the images currently found on Google are from prints from the 60's and 70's, some of them autographed.

However, I'm of the same mind as you, that he cobbled together the perfect composition from a few iconic images. I think the image in my head in that pinakotheken, is a wide shot of an astronaut standing on Earth's surface with a flag in his hand and an Earth rising over his shoulder. You can't see a staff in his hand without it being wide enough, etc.

...ryan

Whole message, with quotes · original

There was no internet on which you could look up all the thousands of then-unreleased Apollo program pictures, there were a few places you could find pictures in hardcopy (Life -- already moribund, National Geographic, a few coffee table books, etc.). 2010-07-02 Dave Lebling replying to Ryan Dunn #

Argues that Wolfe did not need archival research to write the astronaut image passage in The Shadow of the Torturer, citing his stated sources for obscure words. Responds to Ryan Dunn's surprise at the nitpicking by noting that many NASA Apollo prints were only released in recent years, so the picture was hard to find in the 1970s.
disputes · source-and-allusion

He's told us some of his main sources for obscure words, such as "Mrs. Byrne's Dictionary" and books on the Byzantine and Roman Empires. I don't think he needed to spend much time with a microfiche reader, if any.

I also think you overestimate the availability of NASA Apollo prints. Some were only released in the last few years, for example.

But at least we are in violent agreement...

-- Dave

2010-07-02 Ryan Dunn replying to Dave Lebling #

Asks about release dates for NASA images Ryan Dunn has found as possible sources for the astronaut image in Wolfe's work, and lists visual details he says mark it as a composite. Responds to Dave Lebling's claim that Apollo prints were released only recently and that Wolfe's obscure words come from dictionaries rather than microfiche.
questions · source-and-allusion

I'm curious about the release dates of the ones I've found. I've posted a screengrab with my captions on them here:

www.liftingfaces.com/uploads/moonman_sources.jpg

Things to note:

- a strangely stiff banner (i found an image with the flag threaded with a rod along the top) - the deathly desert is reflected in his visor and nothing more, you would see stuff (cameraman) in a real photo - there are images of astronauts with earthrise behind them, but it's an easy enough composite, and symbolic

...ryan

Whole message, with quotes · original
I'm curious about the release dates of the ones I've found. I've posted a screengrab with my captions on them here:

www.liftingfaces.com/uploads/moonman_sources.jpg

Things to note:

- a strangely stiff banner (i found an image with the flag threaded with a rod along the top)
- the deathly desert is reflected in his visor and nothing more, you would see stuff (cameraman) in a real photo
- there are images of astronauts with earthrise behind them, but it's an easy enough composite, and symbolic

...ryan

2010-07-02 Jeff Wilson replying to António Pedro Marques #

Argues that the word 'again' in Rudesind's remark about cleaning the astronaut image refers to an earlier cleaning, so the repeated word is a deliberate pointer rather than a slip. Supports this by quoting the passage where Rudesind says he took the notion to clean it again and describes the blue Urth coming over the astronaut's shoulder.
answers · close-textual-reading · puzzle-solving · theories: Rudesind cleaned the astronaut image twice before

Same as the other 'again', he had cleaned it twice before.

"'Today I took the notion to clean it again. And it needs it?see how nice it's brightening up? There's your blue Urth coming over his shoulder again, fresh as the Autarch's fish.'"

2010-07-02 Jerry Friedman replying to James Wynn #

Argues that humans sit at the bottom of The Wizard Knight's hierarchy, below the Aelf and Overcyns, rather than in the middle. Responds to James Wynn by citing Able's dealings with Disiri, Uri and Baki, and asking how humans fit the deistic order.
disputes · theology-and-religion · close-textual-reading · theories: Humans sit at the bottom of The Wizard Knight's hierarchy

From: James Wynn <[email]>

And what about /The Wizard Knight/? The Aelf could be toys in the hands of evil humans if the humans realized it, just as humans sometimes are to serve the ideals of Valfather and then Michael's > and then discover the ideals that are even higher still.

I agree with that.

He's not a good ruler (although not such a bad one, in Able's judgement), but I don't see that people are enslaved to him--certainly not humanity as a whole.

two levels lower),

Half-dragon? Is that ever made clear?

higher than those that had enslaved humanity) was > also enslaved by those instincts and hence rebelled -- because humans had relinquished the ideals

The Seven Worlds are a picture of a man? Not, maybe, a society?

well be viewed as the most moralistic novel Wolfe has ever written.

For one thing, it's one of the few where sexual fidelity seems like it might be a good idea.

following is
tells Abel that to be a knight he must be
the same authority.

Not to be a knight, unless I'm missing something. All I see is that Michael is answering Able's question of how to find her (TK, Ch. 44).

It's certainly a key scene, and beautifully done, but I get the feeling other scenes show humanity at the bottom. I can't imagine that humans could impress the Overcyns and trick them into worshiping us the way the Aelf have done to some humans, or even pester Overcyns the way Uri and Baki pester Able. Or that any human could transform an Overcyn the way Disiri transforms Able. Both the Aelf and the Overcyns have supernatural powers, but humans don't seem to have any powers that the Aelf don't have (except summoning them). Both the Overcyns and the Aelf have special leaders, such as the Valfather or Disiri, but humanity doesn't--who is the model for Aelf fathers or kings? Able presumably has his priorities as straight as anyone, and his feelings toward the Valfather are very different from his feelings toward Arnthor, but he treats them the same way: respect, obedience, and a little civil disobedience.

So what's going on?

Jerry Friedman

2010-07-02 António Pedro Marques replying to Jeff Wilson #

Challenges Jeff Wilson's claim that the Earth rises over the astronaut's shoulder in the Apollo 17 photograph, joking that allowing rotation makes any reading possible. Responds to Jeff Wilson by ridiculing speculation that Severian's word 'again' implies a video loop or earlier pictures, and noting Severian never mentions them.
disputes · puzzle-solving · close-textual-reading · theories: The Earth over the astronaut's shoulder is a clue to the image's source

Jeff Wilson wrote (02-07-2010 18:16):

But it's far above enough that I think it can be described as being rising over his shoulder
(http://dl.dropbox.com/u/3012885/Var/AS17-134-20387HR-crop.png). The photo is taken sideways anyway. Why mention the shoulder rather than the head? I think saying it was rising over his head would be strange, as it would give no hint of directionality, either 3D or 2D wise. It doesn't say which shoulder, but that's a matter of leaving off irrelevant information (irrelevant for them, who were looking at the picture).

And what do you make of that 'again'? Will someone now suggest they were looking at a video (or an animated gif! or flash animation! or silverlight...) in which the earth rose and set in a loop? Or that they had been watching other pictures of the earth as seen from space? (Aha... we're on to something... that 'again' certainly is a clue that they had been discussing a whole lot of pictures prior to that one... and Severian doesn't mention it... I wonder why...)

Of course, if one allows for rotation, all bets are off. Or on.

Proposes that Rudesind is actually Inire, a hierodule not from Urth, so that his phrase 'your blue Urth' means the world is not his. Responds to Jeff Wilson's suggestion that the phrase derives from the Apollo 17 Earth photograph, offering this as an alternative explanation.
proposes · puzzle-solving · theories: Rudesind is Inire, a hierodule; Inire is not from Urth; 'Your Urth' reflects hierodule origin, not photograph

Either that or Rudesind = Inire, who is likely NOT from Urth, but a hierodule, hence *your* Urth (it ain't his).

Call me crazy.

2010-07-03 Ryan Dunn replying to David Stockhoff #

Proposes that the astronaut figure in the image Rudesind cleaned is Inire in disguise, citing the masks that recur across the books and the idea of hidden identity. Responds to David Stockhoff's list of possible identities and points readers to Mitchell Bailey's earlier archived argument, which he finds convincing, especially its treatment of the Botanic Garden curators.

http://www.urth.net/urth/archives/v0027/0088.shtml

The curators at the Botanic Garden, in particular, makes his argument very convincing to me.

2010-07-03 James Wynn replying to Jerry Friedman #

Argues that The Wizard Knight's Arnthor, his sister, and Garsecg/Setr are dragons in the lower world Muspel, so that the dragon-rule image figures a man ruled by base instincts rather than a society. Grounds this in Able's conquest of his dragon Grengarm, his love for Disiri, and Michael's advice that he must master his desires, and it answers Jerry Friedman's claim that humanity is not enslaved to Arnthor.
proposes · allegory-and-typology · theology-and-religion · theories: Arnthor and his kin are dragons in Muspel; Dragon rule figures humanity ruled by base instincts; Able is us and Mythgarthr is Able; The novel is about people, not Overcyns

He's a figure. Everyone in the story is within his realm IIRC.

I thought it was clear that Arnthor, his sister, and Garsecg/Setr were actually dragons in the lower world, Muspel. They simultaneously exist there as dragons. Humanity Rule by Dragons is a figure, I think, of a man ruled by his base instincts: sex, food, acquisition.

Well, to the extent that society is an extrapolation of the people within it, yeah. But the book is about following "knightly ideals", and one needs a society to act within in order to become a knight. So it works better I think if the figure is individualized. Able is us, in a sense. And Mythgarthr is Able.

(*) I'm putting a mark about this next paragraph to reference it later.

I say the novel is a "figure", because it is the wrong way around to ask "well, were the dragons were doing the wrong thing in attempting to go to Mythgarthr? Were the people of Skai doing the wrong thing in allowing Able to be a hero there?" It's not about them. It's a novel about people not Overcyns.

Well, that's part of it. Able conquers his Dragony baser instincts for the sake of the instincts of Aelfrice -- What those greater instincts are is not clear, but let's say they are Romantic Love and Beauty. Ironically without those Dragony instincts, he would not find the Aelfrice instincts appealing. By the same token, his love for Aelfrice's Disiri causes him to relinquish a chance to be near her and to live in Skai. The lower worlds, in their proper place, drives the hero to the higher worlds.

Well, it's true I'm speaking in terms of the intended message as I see it. Michael says that in order true have Disiri, Able must learn to summon her...that is he must be her her master...that is, he must master his lower desires. In order to do that, he defeats his dragon Grengarm and goes --the opposite direction from Aelfrice-- to Skai. After that, he has become a knight Mythgarthr sense and can perform all the duties of a knight that he has pledged. And *then* he can summon Disiri.

See (*)
Michael is on a quest because he has dishonored himself in some way. So ostensibly it is possible for the people of each level to fail in some way. But it's a novel about people. The Aelf and the Overcyns are devices within that theme.

2010-07-03 Gerry Quinn replying to Jerry Friedman #

Argues that humans in The Wizard Knight lack magic yet hold a central, special place among the worlds, so Mythgartr feels largest despite the hierarchy of worlds. Responds to Jerry Friedman, who had argued that other scenes show humanity at the bottom, citing Aelf and Overcyn powers and leadership.
disputes · theology-and-religion · theories: Mythgartr is central despite humans lacking magic

I agree that the humans seem underpowered in some ways compared to the others. The Aelf have more magic than the humans, and the Dragons have more magic than the Aelf. But the Overcyn have more magic than the humans too! (Wnen I say humans, I mean all on Mythgartr - the Angrborn and the Osterlanders do not have much magic power either.)

But maybe Mythgartr has something special. I know every world is supposed to be smaller than the world above... but I don't feel that about Mythgartr. Maybe it's because the characters are human - but it feels like the largest world in some way.

Whatever the humans have, it ain't magic. But they have something. They are not at the bottom... in some way they are central. And there's another world too, full of humans, this so called 'America'.

- Gerry Quinn

----- Original Message -----
From: Jerry Friedman
It's certainly a key scene, and beautifully done, but I get the feeling other scenes show humanity at the bottom. I can't imagine that humans could impress the Overcyns and trick them into worshiping us the way the Aelf have done to some humans, or even pester Overcyns the way Uri and Baki pester Able. Or that any human could transform an Overcyn the way Disiri transforms Able. Both the Aelf and the Overcyns have supernatural powers, but humans don't seem to have any powers that the Aelf don't have (except summoning them). Both the Overcyns and the Aelf have special leaders, such as the Valfather or Disiri, but humanity doesn't--who is the model for Aelf fathers or kings? Able presumably has his priorities as straight as anyone, and his feelings toward the Valfather are very different from his feelings toward Arnthor, but he treats them the same way: respect, obedience, and a little civil disobedience. http://www.urth.net

2010-07-04 Ryan Dunn replying to António Pedro Marques #

Argues that Father Inire may be Rudesind, pointing to the monkey-like descriptions of Rudesind and Inire and the monkey-like watcher in the jungle. Responds to António Pedro Marques by defending the idea that Inire has a motive to steer Severian's journey toward Yesod, so the link is not mere speculation.
proposes · close-textual-reading · puzzle-solving · theories: Father Inire is Rudesind

The appearance of the Autarch at House Azure opens the gates to other unannounced, unnamed characters appearing. He did it with Hildegrin as well, and a handful of other characters. Why not Inire, perhaps the most talked about yet never seen character??

You sort of proved my point for me.

Why go to the trouble of giving Rudesind monkey-like description? Why have Eata say Inire looks like a monkey? Why have a red, wizened monkey who could be a man were it not for the hand-feet watching over Severian from the jungle above? Why, why WHY?

Because Inire has a clear motive to make sure Severian's journey goes just so, and that he and his eidetic memory make their way to the war with the proper "experiences", for the inevitable trip to Yesod.

No? Not sure that is as speculative as it may seem.

...ryan

I think the reason I seldom remember the english proverb is that it evokes in me the image of a single soul's gradual descending path into damnation. I might remember it if I were going after that image. The portuguese one rather suggests a lot of people with good intentions rather than a single person with a lot of good intentions.

A fabulous thing, intentions.

2010-07-04 António Pedro Marques replying to Ryan Dunn #

Argues against reducing The Book of the New Sun to a single character, rejecting the idea that Father Inire is secretly Rudesind or anyone else unless it yields a richer reading. Grounds this in the Autarch appearing before his identity is known and in a possible Inire sighting at the old Autarch's accident, and urges restraint in speculation.
disputes · puzzle-solving · theories: Father Inire is Rudesind

I don't like the idea of reducing the whole book to a single character. Along the lines of thought that have been presented here, everyone could be everybody (yeah, I know others have complained about this before).

The old Autarch appears in the book before we know who he is. Father Inire is never mentioned as appearing but there is at least one time someone appears who may be he (at the old Autarch's accident). I really don't think any more connections are called for. Where's the added value in positing that FI is, say, Rudesind? Unless it is conducive to a richer reading, I think speculation on any Gene Wolfe material is to be seriously avoided.

Whole message, with quotes · original
Ryan Dunn wrote:
> I tend to favor Inire in disguise, personally (masks abound, etc.). But I am definitely open to Mitchell Bailey's thoughts on the matter from some time back...

I don't like the idea of reducing the whole book to a single character. 
Along the lines of thought that have been presented here, everyone could be

that FI is, say, Rudesind? Unless it is conducive to a richer reading, I

2010-07-05 Eugene Zaretskiy replying to Ryan Dunn #

Argues that the Autarch's appearance at House Azure is a purposeful mask that changes the meaning of ruling the Commonwealth and Severian's trip, and that Inire being Rudesind would add nothing. Rejects the mask-hunting behind the claim, saying the monkey description of Inire is a metaphor that makes him seem non-human to common folk, and asks why Inire would be Rudesind.
disputes · close-textual-reading · puzzle-solving · theories: Inire is Rudesind

I think you may have missed the point, actually. To believe Wolfe places masks everywhere and to search every sentence for signs of these masks is, I believe, a fundamental misunderstanding of why many of Wolfe's characters wear masks. The Autarch appearing in the House Azure before the reader knows its him is not done to make you feel clever when you figure it out. It's done because that fact, when you realize it, fundamentally changes your understanding of what it means to rule to Commonwealth, what it means to be an Autarch, and changes the purpose of Severian's trip there. If I'm being vague, it's because that has been discussed on this list a number of times before in clearer terms than I can provide. More importantly, the details are beside the point.

The point, the one I believe you misinterpreted to believe it supports your point, is that the Autarch's mask in this case serves a very strong purpose that enriches the story. The suggestion that Inire is Rudesind seems to be ignoring this. What value to the book is there if Inire is indeed Rudesind? Saying that it proves Inire is guiding Severian seems ridiculous to me (and perhaps only me) since Rudesind's directions could have been obtained from anyone, and if Inire wanted to guide Severian in meaningful ways, there were many other, better opportunities to have done so. Why search for masks for the sake of masks?

For the same reason any writer has for using metaphors: they provide a convenience when needing to describe someone. In this case, much is accomplished by the use of the word "monkey" without having to assume it's time to go on a meaningless mask-hunt. Personally, I think the description of Inire as a monkey helps build the image the common folk have of him: As being non-human.

Speculation is fine, but as you say: why? Why would Inire be Rudesind?

- EZ

2010-07-05 António Pedro Marques replying to Ryan Dunn #

Responds to Ryan Dunn's claim that Severian's unnamed recurring encounters need not hide identities, such as the Autarch at House Azure and Inire, by questioning what speculation about unrecognized repeat characters adds. Argues that people go unnamed on meeting and are recognised later, comparing it to politicians seen only in newspapers, and demands a purpose for such speculation.
disputes · puzzle-solving · theories: Unnamed characters are secretly the same recurring people

Ryan Dunn wrote (05-07-2010 01:00):

Well, that's just the way it works - people don't usually go around shouting their names on the street. You later on recognise them and think 'oh, I've seen you before'.
And Severian meets those people and later on recognises he's met them. What do we gain at all by positing that he's also met loads of people who were all, in fact, the same as a lot of other people he's met, but doesn't recognise?

How so 'yet'? We talk about powerful politicians all the time, but we seldom meet them in operson, and mostly would never have seen them if not for newspaper photographs and television.

Purpose. Purpose. Where's the added value in such speculation? What richness does it open the door to?

2010-07-05 Jeff Wilson replying to Eugene Zaretskiy #

Argues that Rudesind is Father Inire's body double, using the mask motif of The Book of the New Sun and the directions Rudesind gives Severian that no one else could supply. Answers Eugene Zaretskiy's claim that Inire guiding Severian is ridiculous by citing the armigers' misdirection at Rudesind's first appearance and the crucial directions near the House Absolute.
disputes · puzzle-solving · close-textual-reading · theories: Rudesind is Father Inire's body double; Rudesind's second appearance is Inire's first

There is a very strong mask motif in the BOTNS, so of course we look for a theme of multiple or at least dual identities that would be built or established with that motif. It's not the only theme that a mask motif could support, but others like a protective shell theme don't seem to apply - a couple of the earliest images in the book are the collapsed curtain wall and Eata slipping past the guards at the gate, if anything the BOTNS is about transgressing barriers....which disguises help you do as well.

And on his first appearance, Rudesind delivers directions that are *not* available from anyone, in fact the armigers deliberately misguide him and Rudesind literally sets him on the correct path. This is not the only path, as Rudesind admits the other way might let him make it to Ultan's study after more hours of walking. However, the directions given in his second appearance in the more dangerous proximity of the House Absolute are much more crucial.

Or is Rudesind's second appearance rather Inire's first appearance? I wasn't kidding about Rudesind being Father Inire's body double; it would be very handy to have an alter ego who *can* be in the same room with you at the same time, and the irony of a senile old man calling thousand-year-old Inire old and senile and fumblind with a cover story being commuted into a self-deprecating acknowledgment of his reputation and an admission that he's a complete deception is very Wolfean - and parallels Severian's disembly with Thais, Pega, and Odilo on the raft in URTH.

Whole message, with quotes · original
On 7/5/2010 12:43 AM, Eugene Zaretskiy wrote:
> Saying that it proves Inire is guiding
> Severian seems ridiculous to me (and perhaps only me) since Rudesind's
> directions could have been obtained from anyone, and if Inire wanted
> to guide Severian in meaningful ways, there were many other, better
> opportunities to have done so. Why search for masks for the sake of
> masks?

a theme of multiple or at least dual identities that would be built or

could support, but others like a protective shell theme don't seem to 
apply - a couple of the earliest images in the book are the collapsed 
curtain wall and Eata slipping past the guards at the gate, if anything 
the BOTNS is about transgressing barriers....which disguises help you do 
as well.

And on his first appearance, Rudesind delivers directions that are *not* 
available from anyone, in fact the armigers deliberately misguide him 
and Rudesind literally sets him on the correct path. This is not the 
only path, as Rudesind admits the other way might let him make it to 
Ultan's study after more hours of walking. However, the directions given 
in his second appearance in the more dangerous proximity of the House 
Absolute are much more crucial.

Or is Rudesind's second appearance rather Inire's first appearance? I 
wasn't kidding about Rudesind being Father Inire's body double; it would 
be very handy to have an alter ego who *can* be in the same room with 
you at the same time, and the irony of a senile old man calling 
thousand-year-old Inire old and senile and fumblind with a cover story 
being commuted into a self-deprecating acknowledgment of his reputation 
and an admission that he's a complete deception is very Wolfean - and 
parallels Severian's disembly with Thais, Pega, and Odilo on the raft in 
URTH.

-- 
Jeff Wilson - [email]
IEEE Student Chapter Blog at
< http://ieeetamut.org >

2010-07-05 David Stockhoff replying to John Watkins #

Argues that Rudesind cannot be Inire, the unseen character of the Book of the New Sun, reasoning that Wolfe lets characters reveal themselves through speech and Rudesind's manner and letter do not match Inire's behavior. Responds to Ryan Dunn's suggestion by pointing to Thecla's tale and Inire's described height as contradicting the identification.
disputes · puzzle-solving · close-textual-reading · theories: Rudesind is not Inire

To be fair, Inire's importance-to-visibility ratio is quite high. That is, for a character so vital to the plot---to the plot, not the story---to be so rarely and only indirectly seen is remarkable.

However, I think that itself rules out Rudesind. Wolfe likes to let characters unfold themselves through their speech. Rudesind is right there in plain sight, folksy ways and all. If he were Inire, our only glimpse of Inire's speech and manner when he's not talking to little girls is Rudesind and his letter to Severian. They don't match, and Rudesind doesn't match with Thecla's tale. Inire would have to be an actor, of which there are plenty others. But he's not an actor, and he's got other things to do than risk giving himself away to Severian.

Also, Inire is described as being of middling height or similar. Rudesind is quite tall.

2010-07-05 David Stockhoff replying to Jeff Wilson #

Argues that Rudesind's remarks about a painting of Inire and a painting of himself in The Book of the New Sun suggest the two are doubled, since Rudesind was a boy when the portrait was painted. Grounds the argument in the scene where the Autarch appears as procurer, then Inire, while Rudesind vanishes, which the poster reads as doubling in the scene.
proposes · close-textual-reading · puzzle-solving · theories: Rudesind is Inire, doubled in scene

I forgot about the second time Rudesind appears, when he says something odd about the painting of Inire, but also that there is a painting of himself. He seems confused and excited. But he was a boy when the painting was painted---if he is Inire then Inire was also a boy when his was painted. Fechin painted it. Is that possible?

The Autarch then appears, as the procurer, then Inire, but Rudesind is gone. So doubling arises in the scene.

2010-07-05 Jeff Wilson replying to Ryan Dunn #

Argues that the Curators, who appear at the pinakotheken, library and botanic garden, could all be agents of Inire, an immortal officer of the Autarch's court who vetted thousands of servants as the Autarch's eyes and ears. Responds to Ryan Dunn's suggestion that the Curators are Inire's agents, offering this as a plausible extension of his idea rather than new evidence.
supports · puzzle-solving · theories: The Curators are agents of Inire

It would not be much of a stretch for an immortal officer of the Autarch's court to have vetted and personally charged thousands of servants with being the Autarch's eyes and ears, and helping hands when the need arises.

2010-07-06 Roy C. Lackey replying to Eugene Zaretskiy #

Argues against the theory that Inire is really Rudesind in disguise, using the timing of Severian's last recorded conversation with Rudesind and what Severian says in Urth about his years as autarch. Responds to Eugene Zaretskiy's defence of the theory, asking why a disguised Inire would still need to conceal himself after the New Sun arrives.
disputes · close-textual-reading · chronology-and-plot-logic · theories: Inire is Rudesind in disguise

Indeed.

(I don't know how this topic got switched to this thread.)

As I have already said, the last recorded conversation with Rudesind took place *after* Severian became autarch, and was written ten years after the conversation. Okay, one may argue that Sev becoming autarch wasn't enough (to satisfy the theory that Inire-as-Rudesind was guiding Sev to the throne and toward a New Sun), that Inire must get Sev to Yesod. Fine, let's make that assumption just for the sake of the theory.

Somewhere early in URTH, Sev wrote that he had had few friends while autarch, save for Inire. He also lets drop that Inire had told him that the Cumaean was a Hierodule and it is revealed that the Hierodules BF&O had been his "councilors" (p-354) while he was autarch. It's hard for me to imagine that Sev was not told about Inire's supposed disguises during those ten years, or that he didn't figure it out, but I'll set that aside.

The facts mentioned in the above paragraph come from Sev's second manuscript, written *at least* two generations after the events related in it, which includes the arrival of the New Sun and the transformation of Urth into Ushas. It's a done deed. It's all over with. It's in the history books and can't be undone. There is no need for anyone to play games, no need to maintain past charades that are now meaningless.

If Sev knew, or even suspected, that Rudesind had really been Inire, there was no reason not to say so. If BF&O knew Inire had been an agent of Yesod, there was no reason to withhold that information from him in their meeting in Apu's tomb.

-Roy

Whole message, with quotes · original
Eugene Zaretskiy wrote:
> The point, the one I believe you misinterpreted to believe it supports
> your point, is that the Autarch's mask in this case serves a very
> strong purpose that enriches the story. The suggestion that Inire is
> Rudesind seems to be ignoring this. What value to the book is there if
> Inire is indeed Rudesind? Saying that it proves Inire is guiding
> Severian seems ridiculous to me (and perhaps only me) since Rudesind's
> directions could have been obtained from anyone, and if Inire wanted
> to guide Severian in meaningful ways, there were many other, better
> opportunities to have done so. Why search for masks for the sake of
> masks?
